1.1 The 2-Line Skill Acquisition Formula
                                                                                                                                                                              Deliberate Practice (PC Sandbox)
                                                                                                    Deconstruct (Primitives) + Active Generation (Paper/Docs)                                                    Spaced Retrieval (FIRe + Interleaving) → Automaticity
                                                                                                                                                                                   Zero Reference Lookup

                                                                                                                                                 [Micro-Scaffolded Primitives × High-Signal Exposure] ⋅ Retrieval Friction
                                                                                                                         Mastery Velocity =                                                                                                 [cite: 1, 2, 7]
                                                                                                                                                             Working Memory Overload + Feedback Latency
   • Cognitive Load Optimization (Sweller): Working memory holds only 4–7 chunks simultaneously; looking back at reference material during coding traps syntax in short-term memory and triggers the "vicious cycle of forgetting". Every week breaks complex mechanisms into 3–4 micro-scaﬀolded Knowledge Points (Primitive →
     Worked Example → Immediate Variation → Failure Mode) to build long-term memory schemas without cognitive overload.
   • Automaticity & Freeing Working Memory (Bryan & Harter; Math Academy): Drilling Dart syntax, box constraints, and state ﬂows by hand on paper builds low-level automaticity, freeing conscious working memory during your 1-hour PC block for high-level system architecture.

   • Fractional Implicit Repetition (FIRe) & Non-Interference: Rather than studying two similar concepts simultaneously (which causes associative interference), each week layers orthogonal or hierarchical skills so advanced builds implicitly force retrieval of prior weeks' primitives.

   • Context-Switching Cost Threshold: Because spinning up mental context takes several minutes, never fragment study blocks into intervals shorter than 25–30 minutes.

Modular Active PC Schedule Tiers (Elastic Adaptation)

   • Tier A — Baseline Mode (1 hr/day, Mon–Sat): 50 minutes pure coding/Figma translation from shop notebook + 10 minutes Git commit (feat:, fix:, refactor:) and Obsidian Wiki compilation.

   • Tier B — Split-Shift Boost Mode (2 hrs/day, 3x/week — 1 hr Early Morning + 1 hr Night):

         ◦ Morning Session (60m — Peak Prefrontal Cortex): High cognitive load tasks—implementing core domain logic, state machines, database schemas, or blind coding drills without notes.

         ◦ Night Session (60m — Consolidation & Craft): Visual UI polish, Material 3 token reﬁnement, micro-interactions, widget tests, adversarial AI PR review, and Obsidian Wiki compilation.

   • Tier C — Flex-Sunday Accelerator (4 hrs Active, ~2x/Month) + 45-Min Emergency Fallback:

         ◦ Full 4-Hour Sprint (When Available): Hour 1: 60-minute closed-book Mastery Gate Exam. Hour 2: 45-minute Feynman Blind Diagramming + 15-minute Mistake Log Remediation. Hours 3–4: Deep multi-screen Capstone integration or Obsidian Wiki linting.

         ◦ 45-Minute Festival/Business Emergency Fallback: Complete only the closed-book Mastery Gate (45m compressed slice) so progression gating remains intact without derailing the week.

2.1 Obsidian Vault Directory Architecture

Andrej Karpathy’s LLM Wiki workﬂow treats raw notes as immutable source ﬁles and uses an LLM agent as a compiler that maintains a clean, interlinked markdown encyclopedia.

flutter-mastery-vault/
├── AGENTS.md                                     # Master compiler instructions & schema rules for the LLM
├── index.md                                      # Master MOC (Map of Content) & Knowledge Graph Router
├── log.md                                        # Chronological compilation & mistake audit trail
├── 80_20_parking_lot.md                          # Rabbit-hole topics deferred until needed by a project
├── 00_inbox/                                     # Quick mobile captures during busy shop hours

│   ├── docs_clippings/                           # Obsidian Web Clipper dumps from dart.dev / flutter.dev

    ├── concepts/                                 # Core primitives (e.g., [[Box_Constraints]], [[Isolates]])
    ├── patterns/                                 # Architecture patterns (e.g., [[Offline_Sync_Queue]])
    ├── ui_teardowns/                             # Deconstructions of production apps & Material 3 specs
    └── flashcards/                               # Spaced-repetition Q&A decks generated from bug_logs

2.2 AGENTS.md Schema & Wiki_Concept_Template.md

Save this ﬁle at the root of your Obsidian vault as AGENTS.md:

# AGENTS.md — Obsidian LLM Wiki Compiler Specification

## Core Directive
You are the Knowledge Compiler for this engineering vault. Treat `/raw/` and `/00_inbox/` as immutable source code, and `/wiki/` as the compiled binary output. Never modify files inside `/raw/`.

## Compilation Rules
1. **Atomic Concept Pages:** Every file in `/wiki/concepts/` or `/wiki/patterns/` must represent one distinct primitive or architectural pattern.
2. **Strict Bidirectional Linking:** Every wiki page must link to its prerequisite primitives and dependent patterns using `[[Wiki_Links]]`. Zero orphan pages are permitted.
3. **First-Principles Enforcement:** Every concept page must explicitly define: (a) Source of Truth, (b) Transform Pipeline, (c) Execution Runtime, and (d) I/O Boundaries & Failure Modes.
4. **Contradiction Detection:** When compiling new `/raw/` notes, compare the user's handwritten mental model against existing `/wiki/` pages and official Dart/Flutter specifications. Explicitly flag misconceptions in a `> [!warning] Mental Model Correction` callout.
5. **Index & Log Maintenance:** Every compilation pass must update `index.md` if new nodes were created and append a timestamped entry to `log.md`.

## Standard Output Template (`Wiki_Concept_Template.md`)
---
title: "{{Concept Name}}"
domain: [Dart | Flutter | UI_UX | Architecture | CS_Canon]
week: {{Week_Number}}
mastery_score: {{1-5}}
prerequisites: ["[[Prerequisite_1]]", "[[Prerequisite_2]]"]
---

### 2.3 The Complete Anti-Crutch AI Prompt Toolkit (Mobile + Desktop + Wiki)

#### Prompt 1: Mobile Socratic Tutor & Mental Model Stress-Tester (Passive Shop Window)
```text
System: Act as a Principal Flutter Architect and Cognitive Scientist. Do NOT give me code solutions or write boilerplate for me.
Prompt: "I am in my passive shop window studying [Concept/Primitive, e.g., Flutter Element Tree Reconciliation / Dart StreamControllers].
Here is my current mental model of how it works under the hood:
[Insert voice-to-text or typed explanation]
1. Audit my mental model: Where is it architecturally, mechanically, or memory-wise inaccurate?
2. What edge case or runtime scenario breaks my assumption?
3. Ask me 3 sequential diagnostic questions (one at a time) to force me to discover the missing piece myself."

Prompt 2: Handwritten Paper-Code & Widget Tree Grader (Passive Shop Window)

System: Act as a strict Dart Compiler and Senior Flutter Reviewer. Do NOT rewrite my code for me.
Prompt: "I wrote this Dart logic / Widget tree by hand in my physical notebook without an IDE [Upload photo or paste transcription]:
1. Act as the Dart static analyzer: Point out line-by-line where syntax, null-safety, or type errors exist, but make me state the fix.
2. Act as the Flutter layout engine: Trace the BoxConstraints down and Sizes up for my sketched widget tree. Will this throw an unbounded constraint error (e.g., RenderFlex overflow)?
3. Grade my paper architecture from 1–5 on readiness for my 1-hour PC coding block."

Prompt 3: Staﬀ UI/UX Visual & Accessibility Critique (Passive Shop Window)

System: Act as a Staff Product Designer specializing in Material 3, iOS HIG, and Gestalt psychology. Do not redesign the screen for me.
Prompt: "Review my handwritten wireframe / Figma layout description for [Screen Name]:
1. Audit visual hierarchy, 8pt grid alignment, optical balance, and touch target sizing (minimum 48x48dp).
2. Identify missing UI states (Idle, Loading, Empty, Error, Offline, Partial Data).
3. Audit Accessibility (AX Tree announcement order, contrast ratios, screen reader focus traps).
Give me 3 concrete design questions I must resolve on paper before opening my IDE."

Prompt 4: Senior Architect Adversarial PR Reviewer (Active PC Window — Last 10 Mins)

System: Act as a Principal Mobile Security & Performance Engineer. Do NOT refactor my code for me.

Section 5: Granular Weekly Blueprints — Phase 1 (Weeks 1–6)
Week 1: Dart Type System, Sound Null Safety, Memory Models & Control Flow Primitives

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Every variable in Dart is an immutable or mutable binding pointing to an object allocated on the Heap (within an Isolate's private memory space), governed at compile-time and runtime by a sound type system where null is detached from the top type hierarchy (Object? vs Object).

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): None (Entry Node). Baseline familiarity with opening a terminal and text editor.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points (Primitive → Concept → Worked Example → Failure Mode)

   1. KP 1: Value Semantics vs. Reference Bindings (final vs. const vs. var)

        ◦ Primitive: Stack pointers vs. Heap objects and canonicalized compile-time constants.

        ◦ Concept: final locks the pointer reference at runtime (the underlying heap collection can still mutate); const freezes both the pointer and the transitive object graph at compile time and canonicalizes identical instances to a single memory address.

        ◦ Worked Example:

           final listA = [1, 2];
           listA.add(3); // VALID: reference didn't change, heap object mutated.
           const listB = [1, 2];
           // listB.add(3); // THROWS UnsupportedError at runtime: Cannot add to an unmodifiable list.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Assuming a final List<String> inside a state class is immutable, mutating it in place (state.items.add(x)), and wondering why Flutter/Riverpod fails to detect a state change (because identical(oldList, newList) is still true).

   2. KP 2: Sound Null Safety & Type Promotion (T vs. T?)

        ◦ Primitive: Bifurcated type hierarchy (Object has non-nullable subtypes; Null is a separate branch under Object?).

        ◦ Concept: Flow-sensitive type analysis promotes a nullable local variable T? to non-nullable T after a null-check guard (if (x != null)), provided the variable is local, private/non-overridable (Dart 3.2+ ﬁeld promotion), and not mutated by a closure.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Overusing the bang operator (!) on external data or attempting ﬂow promotion on a public mutable class property, triggering a Null check operator used on a null value runtime crash.

   3. KP 3: Strict Operators, Guard Clauses & Control Flow Expressions

        ◦ Primitive: Null-aware coalescing (??, ??=, ?.) and early-return guard clauses.

        ◦ Concept: Flattening nested if/else pyramids into linear top-to-bottom guard clauses so invalid states exit immediately.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Deeply nested arrow anti-patterns (if (a != null) { if (b != null) { ... } }) that exhaust working memory during code reading.

Passive Shop Protocol (2–4 hrs/day, Mon–Sat)

   • Oﬃcial Docs to Read (dart.dev): Language → Basics (Variables, Operators, Control Flow); Language → Types (Built-in types, Typedefs); Understanding Null Safety (whole diagnostic guide).

   • 30-Min Book Reading Assignment: Think Like a Programmer — Chapter 1 (Strategies for Problem Solving) & Chapter 2 (Pure Puzzles). Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Always restate a problem in terms of explicit state transitions and constraints before writing a loop."

   • Physical Notebook & Active Generation Drills (Paper Only):

        ◦ Draw two memory diagrams (Stack vs. Heap) showing what happens in memory when final a = [1, 2]; final b = a; b.add(3); executes versus const c = [1, 2]; const d = [1, 2];.

        ◦ Handwrite a Dart function parseRetailReceipt(String? rawInput) that validates a messy string, extracts item prices using string manipulation, handles null/malformed lines safely without !, and calculates tax and discounts using early-return guard clauses.

   • Obsidian /raw/ Capture & Mobile AI Sparring:

        ◦ Run Prompt 1 (Socratic Tutor) on your phone: "Interrogate my understanding of why Dart's null safety is called 'sound' compared to TypeScript's null checks, and test me on when ﬂow analysis refuses to promote a nullable variable."

Active PC Protocol (Modular Tiers)

   • Tier A — Baseline (1 hr/day, Mon–Sat):

        ◦ Day 1: Initialize Git repo dart-core-foundations. Conﬁgure strict analysis_options.yaml (strict-casts: true, strict-inference: true, strict-raw-types: true). Translate your paper memory-model tests (identical(), hashCode) into bin/memory_model.dart.

        ◦ Day 2: Implement parseRetailReceipt from your shop notebook. Deliberate Error: Trigger a closure variable mutation that breaks Dart's ﬂow-analysis type promotion, observe the compiler error, and ﬁx it using a local shadow variable.

        ◦ Day 3: Build a CLI Currency & Tax Rounding Engine handling ﬂoating-point precision traps (why 0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3 in IEEE 754 double vs. integer minor units / paise / cents).

        ◦ Day 4: Implement custom Exception types and input sanitizers for CLI arguments (stdin).

        ◦ Day 5: Build Weekly Mini-Deliverable: A interactive CLI Retail Cash Register & Day-Book Calculator that reads transaction strings, rejects corrupt entries, and prints a formatted ASCII summary table.

        ◦ Day 6: Zero-AI stress test: Add discount tiers and GST tax splits completely oﬄine with docs closed.

   • Tier B — Split-Shift Boost (3x/week: 1 hr Morning + 1 hr Night):

        ◦ Morning (60m): Write pure algorithmic drills from Think Like a Programmer Ch 2 (Luhn credit-card checksum validator in strict Dart) without looking at notes.

        ◦ Night (60m): Write unit tests using package:test for all edge cases (empty string, negative numbers, null inputs) and run Prompt 5 (Obsidian Wiki Compiler) to compile [[Dart_Sound_Null_Safety]] and [[Stack_vs_Heap_Canonicalization]].

   • Tier C — Flex-Sunday Accelerator (4 hrs, or 45m Fallback):

        ◦ Full 4-Hr: Complete Week 1 Mastery Gate (60m) + Feynman Blind Diagramming of Dart Memory & Type Hierarchy (45m) + Extend the CLI Cash Register to persist daily summaries to a local .csv ﬁle using dart:io (135m).

        ◦ 45-Min Fallback: Complete the Mastery Gate Exam only.

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

   1. Q: Why does identical(const [1, 2], const [1, 2]) evaluate to true, while identical(finalListA, finalListB) with the same elements evaluates to false? A: const canonicalizes collections at compile time into a single shared memory address on the heap; final allocates a distinct heap object on each invocation.

   2. Q: Under what 3 conditions will the Dart compiler refuse to promote a nullable variable int? x after if (x != null)? A: (1) x is a public property or getter that could be overridden by a subclass; (2) x is mutated inside a closure that might execute later; (3) x is an external variable mutated elsewhere.

   3. Q: Why must ﬁnancial currency calculations never use double directly for addition/subtraction? A: double uses base-2 IEEE 754 ﬂoating-point representation, which cannot represent base-10 fractions like 0.1 exactly, causing cumulative rounding drift. Store minor units as int (e.g., paise/cents).

   4. Q: What is the diﬀerence between late final int x; and int? x;? A: int? allows null at runtime and enforces compile-time null checks; late final defers initialization to runtime, strips compile-time checks, and throws a LateInitializationError if read before assignment.

   5. Q: What is the top type of all non-nullable types vs. all types in Dart? A: Object is the root of all non-nullable types; Object? is the root of all types (the union of Object and Null).

   • Interleaved Task: Baseline Week (Interleaving starts Week 2).

The Mastery Gate (Blind Exit Exam — 60 Minutes, Zero-AI, Closed-Book)

   • Challenge: On a blank .dart ﬁle with internet and AI disabled, build a Retail Inventory SKU Validator & Invoice Totaler. It must accept a raw list of messy strings ("SKU-104 | Qty: 3 | Price: 450.50 | Discount: 10%", including corrupted lines, missing discounts, and negative quantities), parse them into integer paise/cents,
     compute net totals, and output both valid invoice lines and a structured error report for rejected lines.

   • 1–5 Grading Rubric (Pass = Average ≥ 4/5, no category < 3/5):

        ◦ Concept (1–5): Drawn stack/heap diagram and null-promotion explanation on paper are 100% accurate.

        ◦ Implementation (1–5): Zero ! bang operators used; zero double rounding bugs; compiles with strict-casts and zero linter warnings.

        ◦ Design/Structure (1–5): Uses ﬂat guard clauses (max indentation depth ≤ 2) and clean Conventional Commits.

        ◦ Debugging (1–5): Gracefully catches and categorizes all 5 malformed input edge cases without throwing unhandled exceptions.

Week 2: Functions, Lexical Closures, OOP vs. Composition & Collection Pipelines

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Data transformations should ﬂow through pure, immutable collection pipelines (Iterable<T> lazy evaluation), while domain entities should model behavior via interfaces (implements), mixins (with), and composition rather than brittle, deep inheritance hierarchies (extends).

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Week 1 Mastery Gate (≥ 4/5 on Sound Null Safety, Stack/Heap memory, and strict types).

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

   1. KP 1: First-Class Functions & Lexical Closures

        ◦ Primitive: Function pointers bundled with their surrounding lexical environment scope on the heap.

        ◦ Concept: When an inner function references a variable from an outer function, Dart allocates that variable on a heap context frame so it survives after the outer function returns.

        ◦ Worked Example:

           IntPredicate makeThresholdFilter(int minAmount) {
             return (int value) => value >= minAmount; // Captures `minAmount` in lexical closure
           }

        ◦ Failure Mode: Unintentionally capturing a heavy object (like a BuildContext or large controller) inside a long-lived closure or listener, preventing the Garbage Collector from freeing it (memory leak).

   2. KP 2: Lazy Iterable<T> Pipelines vs. Eager List<T> Allocations

        ◦ Primitive: Pull-based iterator state machines (moveNext(), current).

        ◦ Concept: Chaining .where(...).map(...) does not execute or allocate intermediate lists until a terminal operation (.toList(), .fold(), for-in) pulls values through the pipeline.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Storing a lazy Iterable created by .map() in a ﬁeld and iterating over it multiple times in the UI, causing expensive transformation functions to re-run on every access because .toList() was omitted.

   3. KP 3: Composition, implements, mixin, and Value Equality (operator == & hashCode)

        ◦ Primitive: Virtual method tables (vtables), implicit interfaces, and hash-bucket lookup contracts.

        ◦ Concept: In Dart, every class implicitly deﬁnes an interface. Prefer composing small capability mixins (mixin Auditable) and implementing explicit contracts (implements PaymentGateway) over deep extends chains. Override operator == and hashCode (Object.hash(...)) + copyWith for value semantics.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Overriding operator == on a domain model without overriding hashCode (or using mutable ﬁelds in hashCode), causing objects to vanish when placed inside a Set or used as keys in a Map.

Passive Shop Protocol (2–4 hrs/day, Mon–Sat)

   • Oﬃcial Docs to Read (dart.dev): Language → Functions; Language → Classes & Objects (Classes, Constructors, Methods, Extend a class, Mixins); Core Libraries → Collections (Iterable collections guide).

   • 30-Min Book Reading Assignment: Think Like a Programmer — Chapter 3 (Solving Problems with Arrays & Data Transformations). Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Identify when a multi-pass loop can be expressed as a single declarative ﬁlter-map-reduce pipeline."

   • Physical Notebook & Active Generation Drills (Paper Only):

        ◦ Handwrite an immutable ProductItem class from memory including final ﬁelds, a const constructor, copyWith, operator ==, and hashCode using Object.hash.

        ◦ Trace on paper step-by-step the exact execution order of items.where((x) => ...).map((x) => ...).take(2).toList() on a 5-element list, proving why elements 3, 4, and 5 are never passed to .map().

   • Obsidian /raw/ Capture & Mobile AI Sparring:

        ◦ Run Prompt 2 (Paper-Code Grader) on a photo of your handwritten copyWith and operator == implementation, asking the AI to test how your copyWith handles resetting a nullable ﬁeld back to null.

Active PC Protocol (Modular Tiers)

   • Tier A — Baseline (1 hr/day, Mon–Sat):

        ◦ Day 1: Build lexical closure factories (conﬁgurable validators, rate-limiters, and memoization wrappers) in Dart.
        ◦ Day 2: Write benchmarks comparing lazy Iterable chaining vs. eager .toList() intermediate allocations over 100,000 retail transactions.

        ◦ Day 3: Implement a domain hierarchy using abstract interface class (Dart 3 class modiﬁers), mixin, and composition. Deliberate Error: Put a class with a broken hashCode (returning a random int or omitting it while overriding ==) into a Set, observe duplicate insertion bugs, and ﬁx it.

        ◦ Day 4: Implement the Closure-Wrapper trick (T Function()? or sentinel object) inside copyWith so nullable ﬁelds can be explicitly set back to null.

        ◦ Day 5: Build Weekly Mini-Deliverable: An in-memory Retail Analytics & Customer Loyalty Engine using pure fold, where, expand, and Map grouping (zero for loops allowed).

        ◦ Day 6: Zero-AI refactoring and unit testing of all collection pipelines.

   • Tier B — Split-Shift Boost (3x/week: 1 hr Morning + 1 hr Night):

        ◦ Morning (60m): Implement custom generic collection extensions (extension IterableX<T> on Iterable<T>) for groupBy, distinctBy, and sumBy from scratch without packages.

        ◦ Night (60m): Write exhaustive unit tests for groupBy and compile Obsidian Wiki nodes [[Lazy_Iterable_Evaluation]], [[Value_Equality_and_HashCode]], and [[Dart_Class_Modifiers]].

   • Tier C — Flex-Sunday Accelerator (4 hrs, or 45m Fallback):

        ◦ Full 4-Hr: Week 2 Mastery Gate (60m) + Feynman Blind Diagramming of Lexical Closures & Hash Buckets (45m) + Combine Week 1's CLI Parser with Week 2's Analytics Engine into a uniﬁed query tool (135m).

        ◦ 45-Min Fallback: Complete Week 2 Mastery Gate Exam.

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why can a standard copyWith({String? note}) method not set this.note back to null if it uses note ?? this.note? A: Because passing note: null is indistinguishable from omitting the parameter (null ?? this.note always falls back to the old non-null value). You must use a function wrapper String? Function()? note
     or a const Object _sentinel.

  2. Q: What is the invariant contract between a == b and a.hashCode == b.hashCode? A: If a == b is true, then a.hashCode == b.hashCode MUST be true (though two unequal objects may share a hash collision).

  3. Q: How many elements are evaluated by .map() in [1, 2, 3, 4, 5].map(expensiveFn).first? A: Exactly 1 element (1), because Iterable.map is lazy and .first stops pulling after the ﬁrst element.

  4. Q: What is the diﬀerence between extends, implements, and with in Dart? A: extends inherits implementation and single-parent state; implements enforces the public interface contract only (zero inherited implementation); with mixes in reusable method/ﬁeld bodies horizontally without deep subclassing.

  5. Q: How does a closure cause a memory leak in a UI application? A: If a closure registered to a long-lived stream or global service captures this (a short-lived screen/widget), the entire screen graph stays reachable from GC roots.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Refactor your Week 1 SKU Invoice Parser so all string ﬁltering, tax aggregation, and error partitioning use a single immutable .fold() pass over immutable value-equal domain models.

Week 3: Modern Dart 3 (Records, Patterns, Exhaustive Sealed Classes) & Asynchronous Primitives

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Invalid runtime states should be made unrepresentable at compile time using Algebraic Data Types (sealed class + exhaustive switch expressions), while all asynchronous I/O runs on a single-threaded Event Loop divided into a high-priority Microtask Queue and a standard Event Queue.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1 & 2 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: Algebraic Data Types (sealed class), Records & Destructuring Patterns

        ◦ Primitive: Closed type hierarchies and structural tuple bundling ((int, {String name})).

        ◦ Concept: Marking a base class sealed restricts all subtypes to the same library and enables the compiler to verify exhaustiveness in switch expressions—replacing fragile boolean ﬂags (isLoading, hasError, data != null) with mathematically sound state machines.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          sealed class SyncState<T> { const SyncState(); }
          final class SyncInitial<T> extends SyncState<T> { const SyncInitial(); }
          final class SyncLoading<T> extends SyncState<T> { const SyncLoading(); }
          final class SyncSuccess<T> extends SyncState<T> { final T data; const SyncSuccess(this.data); }
          final class SyncFailure<T> extends SyncState<T> { final String message; const SyncFailure(this.message); }

          String render<T>(SyncState<T> state) => switch (state) {
             SyncInitial() => 'Idle',
             SyncLoading() => 'Syncing...',
             SyncSuccess(:final data) => 'Loaded: $data',
             SyncFailure(:final message) when message.isEmpty => 'Unknown error',
             SyncFailure(:final message) => 'Error: $message',
          };

        ◦ Failure Mode: Using a default: or _ wildcard branch inside a switch over a sealed class, which silently disables compiler exhaustiveness errors when a new state subtype is added later.

  2. KP 2: The Single-Threaded Dart Event Loop, Future<T>, and async/await

        ◦ Primitive: Call Stack → Microtask Queue (FIFO, drained completely ﬁrst) → Event Queue (I/O, Timers, UI gestures).

        ◦ Concept: async/await is syntactic sugar over Future.then state machines; at every await, execution yields back to the Event Loop so the thread can process UI frames or other events, resuming in the Microtask queue once the Future completes.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Using an async lambda inside List.forEach (items.forEach((x) async => await save(x))), which ﬁres all futures concurrently without awaiting them, causing unhandled race conditions (always use for (final x in items) { await save(x); } for sequential or Future.wait(...) for parallel).

  3. KP 3: Reactive Stream<T>, async* Generators & StreamController Lifecycle

        ◦ Primitive: Asynchronous push-based sequences (Single-Subscription vs. Broadcast streams).

        ◦ Concept: async* functions use yield to emit values over time cleanly; manual StreamControllers require explicit .close() and StreamSubscription.cancel() management to prevent memory leaks.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Listening twice to a single-subscription stream (Bad state: Stream has already been listened to) or failing to cancel a StreamSubscription when a component is disposed.

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why can holding three booleans (bool isLoading, bool isError, bool isSuccess) represent impossible UI states, and how do sealed classes solve this? A: Three booleans allow 23 = 8 combinations (e.g., isLoading == true && isError == true simultaneously). A sealed class with 4 subtypes restricts the state space to
     exactly 4 mutually exclusive states veriﬁed at compile time.

  2. Q: What happens to the Event Queue if a microtask continuously schedules another microtask? A: The Event Queue is completely starved (blocked forever) because the Dart runtime drains the Microtask Queue to empty before processing the next single item in the Event Queue.

  3. Q: Why does adding a default: branch to a switch statement over a sealed class weaken type safety? A: It suppresses the compiler's exhaustiveness check, so if you add a 5th subclass to the sealed class next month, the compiler won't warn you where you forgot to handle it.

  4. Q: What is the diﬀerence between a Single-Subscription Stream and a Broadcast Stream? A: A Single-Subscription stream buﬀers events until a single listener attaches and throws if listened to twice (ideal for ﬁle/network I/O); a Broadcast stream allows multiple listeners simultaneously and drops events if no one is listening (ideal for UI event
     buses).

  5. Q: How do you destructure a Map<String, dynamic> safely in Dart 3 to extract 'user': {'name': String name, 'age': int age} only when age >= 18? A: if (json case {'user': {'name': final String name, 'age': final int age}} when age >= 18) { ... }

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Combine Week 1 (integer currency safety), Week 2 (lazy Iterable + value equality), and Week 3 (sealed states + Streams) to stream deduplicated, validated retail transactions through a state machine.

Week 4: UI/UX Visual Foundations, Gestalt Psychology, 8pt Spatial Grids & Figma Design Token Pipelines

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: High-craft UI is not subjective artistic decoration; it is applied cognitive psychology and mathematical constraint systems—governing visual hierarchy through luminance contrast, typography scales, Gestalt grouping laws, and strict 8pt spatial grids mapped 1:1 from Paper → Figma Variables → Flutter
     Design Tokens.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–3 Mastery Gates (Dart Core Mastery complete).

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: Visual Hierarchy, Gestalt Laws (Proximity, Similarity, Common Region) & Optical Alignment

        ◦ Primitive: Human visual cortex pre-attentive processing (< 200ms scan).

        ◦ Concept: Users do not read screens top-to-bottom; they scan by luminance weight, size, and spatial grouping. Law of Proximity: The spacing inside a component (e.g., 4px/8px between label and value) must always be strictly smaller than the spacing between distinct components (e.g., 16px/24px) so the eye groups related elements
          automatically without needing heavy borders everywhere.

        ◦ Worked Example: De-emphasize secondary metadata using softer color contrast (muted grey) and smaller font weight rather than competing with the primary metric's bold high-contrast headline.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Putting equal font sizes, equal weights, and 1px black borders around every single box on a screen, creating visual noise where nothing stands out as the primary action.

  2. KP 2: The 8pt Spatial Grid, Touch Targets (48x48dp) & Fluid Modular Type Scales

        ◦ Primitive: Device-independent pixels (dp / logical pixels) and base-8 geometric progressions (4, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 48, 64).

        ◦ Concept: Every margin, padding, icon size, and container height snaps to multiples of 8dp (with 4dp reserved for tight internal icon-to-text gaps). All interactive touch targets must maintain a minimum hit area of 48x48dp (Material 3) / 44x44pt (iOS HIG) even if the visual icon is only 20x20dp.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Arbitrary magic numbers (padding: 13.5, fontSize: 15, height: 29) that cause jagged baseline misalignment across devices and frustrating missed taps on mobile.

  3. KP 3: Perceptual Color Systems (HSL / OKLCH), Semantic Color Roles & The 5 Mandatory UI States

        ◦ Primitive: Lightness/Chroma/Hue channels and semantic token aliasing ( Primitive Token: Blue-600 → Semantic Token: colorSurfacePrimary / colorOnSurfaceVariant).

        ◦ Concept: Never hardcode hex values (#3B82F6) directly on a widget. Map primitive palettes into semantic roles (surface, surfaceContainerHigh, onSurface, primary, error) that automatically invert luminance contrast for Dark Mode while maintaining WCAG AA contrast (≥ 4.5 : 1 for body text, ≥ 3 : 1 for large text/UI controls).
          Every screen must explicitly design 5 states: Idle/Empty, Loading (Skeleton), Populated, Partial/Oﬄine, and Error.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Designing only the "Happy Path" with perfect mock text, or inverting black to pure #000000 and white to #FFFFFF in Dark Mode without adjusting surface elevation luminance (in Dark Mode, higher elevation surfaces get lighter, not darker with drop shadows).

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why do drop shadows fail to communicate surface elevation in Dark Mode, and how does Material 3 solve this? A: Against a dark background, black drop shadows are nearly invisible. Material 3 communicates elevation in Dark Mode by making higher surfaces progressively lighter (using surface container tonal overlays:
     surfaceContainerLowest → surfaceContainerHighest).

  2. Q: What is the Gestalt Law of Proximity in terms of exact pixel math on a card component? A: Internal padding and element spacing within a card (e.g., 4dp between title and subtitle, 12dp card padding) must be noticeably smaller than the outer margin between adjacent cards (e.g., 16dp or 24dp) so items inside the card bind together visually.

  3. Q: Why must ﬁnancial or numerical tables always use tabularFigures (FontFeature.tabularFigures())? A: Proportional fonts give digits like 1 and 8 diﬀerent widths, causing decimal points and columns to zig-zag and shift horizontally during live updates. Tabular ﬁgures force every digit onto an identical monospace width.

  4. Q: What are the minimum WCAG 2.1 AA contrast ratios and touch target dimensions for mobile UI? A: Contrast: ≥ 4.5 : 1 for normal text (< 18pt), ≥ 3.0 : 1 for large text and active UI components/icons. Touch targets: minimum 48 × 48dp (Android/M3) or 44 × 44pt (iOS).

  5. Q: How do Figma Auto-Layout's three sizing modes (Fixed, Hug Contents, Fill Container) map conceptually to Flutter layout widgets? A: Fixed → SizedBox(width: x); Hug Contents → MainAxisSize.min / shrink-wrapping child size; Fill Container → Expanded or double.infinity within bounded constraints.

Week 5: Flutter Engine Architecture (The 3 Trees) & The Box Constraint Protocol

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Widgets are cheap, immutable conﬁguration blueprints; the Element tree manages persistent component identity, state lifecycle, and tree reconciliation in memory; and the RenderObject tree executes layout and painting under a single unbreakable law: "Constraints go down. Sizes go up. Parent sets
     position.".

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–4 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: The Three-Tree Architecture (Widget → Element → RenderObject) & Keys

        ◦ Primitive: Immutable conﬁguration structs (Widget), mutable lifecycle nodes (ComponentElement / RenderObjectElement), and heavy layout/raster objects (RenderBox).

        ◦ Concept: Calling setState() or rebuilding a widget does not destroy and recreate the screen. Flutter runs Widget.canUpdate(oldWidget, newWidget) (checking oldWidget.runtimeType == newWidget.runtimeType && oldWidget.key == newWidget.key). If true, the existing Element and RenderObject are mutated in
          place (updateRenderObject).

        ◦ Worked Example:

          Widget Tree (Immutable Blueprint)      Element Tree (Lifecycle/Identity)      RenderObject Tree (Layout/Paint)
          +-----------------------+              +--------------------------+           +--------------------------+
          | Padding (const)       | -----------> | SingleChildRenderElement | --------> | RenderPadding            |
          +-----------------------+   mount /    +--------------------------+ creates +--------------------------+
                      |               update                  |                                      |
          +-----------------------+              +--------------------------+           +--------------------------+
          | CustomCounterStateful | -----------> | StatefulElement (State) |            | (No RenderObject;        |
          +-----------------------+              +--------------------------+           | delegates to child)      |

        ◦ Failure Mode: Reordering, inserting, or removing stateful widgets of the same runtimeType inside a Column/ListView without a ValueKey/ObjectKey. Because runtimeType matches at index 0, the Element tree keeps the old State attached to the wrong data item!

  2. KP 2: The Box Constraint Protocol (BoxConstraints: Tight vs. Loose vs. Unbounded)

        ◦ Primitive: 4 doubles passed down the RenderBox tree: minWidth, maxWidth, minHeight, maxHeight.

        ◦ Concept:

             ▪ Tight Constraints (minWidth == maxWidth): The parent forces the exact size (e.g., root FlutterView or SizedBox.expand). Child's desired size is ignored unless wrapped in an alignment widget (Center, Align) that converts tight constraints into Loose Constraints (0.0 <= width <= maxWidth).

             ▪ Unbounded Constraints (maxHeight == double.infinity): Passed down the main axis by scrolling viewports (ListView, SingleChildScrollView) or ﬂex containers (Row/Column) before ﬂex allocation.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Placing an Expanded (flex > 0) or a vertical ListView inside another vertical Column or SingleChildScrollView without bounded height—triggering the classic Vertical viewport was given unbounded height or RenderFlex children have non-zero flex but incoming height constraints are
          unbounded crash.

  3. KP 3: Flex Layout Algorithm (Row / Column / Flex / Expanded / Flexible / IntrinsicHeight)

        ◦ Primitive: The 2-pass RenderFlex layout algorithm: Pass 1 measures non-ﬂex children (flex == 0) with unbounded main-axis constraints; Pass 2 divides remaining main-axis space (maxMainSize - allocatedSpace) proportionally among Flexible/Expanded children.

        ◦ Concept: Flexible(fit: FlexFit.loose) allows the child to be up to its share of remaining space; Expanded (FlexFit.tight) forces the child to ﬁll 100% of its remaining share.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Using IntrinsicHeight or IntrinsicWidth inside a scrolling list (ListView.builder). Intrinsic widgets require a speculative pre-layout pass over all children (O(N 2 ) layout cost), destroying 60/120fps scroll performance.

Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Physical and visual constraints prevent user error before it occurs; layout constraints prevent rendering ambiguity."

          min..max, h: min..max) passed down each arrow and the exact Size(w, h) returned up each arrow.

        ◦ Draw the exact 2-step tree diagram showing what happens in the Widget tree and Element tree when two stateful list items [Item A, Item B] swap positions without keys versus with ValueKey(id).

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why does Scaffold(body: Container(width: 100, height: 100, color: Colors.red)) ﬁll the entire screen instead of rendering a 100 × 100 box? A: Scaffold passes tight constraints matching the screen size down to its body. Container must obey incoming tight constraints, so its 100 × 100 preference is clamped to the
     screen's minWidth/minHeight. Wrapping it in Center or Align loosens the constraints (minWidth: 0, minHeight: 0), allowing the 100 × 100 size to take eﬀect.

  2. Q: What is the exact implementation of Widget.canUpdate(Widget oldWidget, Widget newWidget) in the Flutter framework? A: return oldWidget.runtimeType == newWidget.runtimeType && oldWidget.key == newWidget.key;

  3. Q: Why does placing a Row(children: [Text('Very long text...')]) overﬂow instead of wrapping the text onto a second line? A: Row measures its non-ﬂex children by passing unbounded horizontal constraints (0.0 <= width <= double.infinity). Given inﬁnite max width, RenderParagraph lays out the text on a single long
     line, which then exceeds the Row's actual screen width. Wrapping Text in Expanded or Flexible passes bounded maxWidth to Text.

  4. Q: Why is splitting a big widget method _buildHeader() into a separate class _Header extends StatelessWidget with a const constructor superior for performance? A: Helper methods (_buildHeader()) execute and rebuild their entire subtree every time the parent calls build(). A separate StatelessWidget creates its own
     Element boundary; if marked const, Flutter sees identical(oldWidget, newWidget) and skips rebuilding the entire subtree in O(1) time.

  5. Q: When should you use a GlobalKey versus a ValueKey, and why are GlobalKeys expensive? A: ValueKey is local to a single parent's child list reconciliation and is very cheap. GlobalKey registers the Element in a global map across the entire app (allowing state preservation when moving a widget across diﬀerent parents or accessing
     RenderBox / FormState), which carries high memory and tree-reparenting overhead.

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: A production UI must adapt ﬂuidly to any viewport constraint (LayoutBuilder local constraints vs. MediaQuery global device metrics), inject custom semantic design tokens via inherited ThemeExtension<T> trees, and project a clean, navigable Semantics (Accessibility) Tree to OS screen readers and
     hardware keyboards.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–5 Mastery Gates.
3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

   1. KP 1: Custom Design Token Injection via ThemeExtension<T> & InheritedWidget Mechanics

        ◦ Primitive: O(1) upward context lookup (context.dependOnInheritedWidgetOfExactType<T>()).

        ◦ Concept: Every Element maintains a hash map of active InheritedElement ancestors. Extending ThemeExtension<AppColorsExtension> (implementing copyWith and lerp using Color.lerp) allows your custom Week 4 Figma semantic tokens to integrate natively into
          Theme.of(context).extension<AppColorsExtension>()! with smooth animated transitions between Light and Dark modes.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          @immutable
          class AppSpacingTokens extends ThemeExtension<AppSpacingTokens> {
            final double sm; final double md; final double lg;
            const AppSpacingTokens({this.sm = 8.0, this.md = 16.0, this.lg = 24.0});
            @override
            AppSpacingTokens copyWith({double? sm, double? md, double? lg}) =>
                 AppSpacingTokens(sm: sm ?? this.sm, md: md ?? this.md, lg: lg ?? this.lg);
            @override
            AppSpacingTokens lerp(ThemeExtension<AppSpacingTokens>? other, double t) {
              if (other is! AppSpacingTokens) return this;
              return AppSpacingTokens(
                 sm: lerpDouble(sm, other.sm, t)!,
                 md: lerpDouble(md, other.md, t)!,
                 lg: lerpDouble(lg, other.lg, t)!,
              );
            }
          }

        ◦ Failure Mode: Calling MediaQuery.of(context) instead of MediaQuery.sizeOf(context) at the top of a large screen. MediaQuery.of(context) subscribes the widget to every MediaQuery property (including keyboard viewInsets animating 60 times a second), forcing the entire screen subtree to rebuild on every frame of the
          keyboard animation! Always use speciﬁc aspect selectors (MediaQuery.sizeOf, MediaQuery.paddingOf).

   2. KP 2: Adaptive & Responsive Architecture (LayoutBuilder vs. MediaQuery.sizeOf)

        ◦ Primitive: Breakpoint-driven structural branching (< 600dp Compact/Phone → NavigationBar; 600–840dp Medium/Tablet → NavigationRail; > 840dp Expanded/Desktop → NavigationDrawer + Master-Detail Split View).

        ◦ Concept: Use MediaQuery.sizeOf(context) only for top-level app shell decisions; use LayoutBuilder inside reusable components so a component adapts to the actual local parent constraints it is given, regardless of the overall screen width.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Designing a phone-only single-column layout that stretches buttons and text lines to 1200px wide on a tablet, foldable, or desktop window (violating the 60–75 character optimal reading measure).

   3. KP 3: The Flutter Accessibility (Semantics) Tree (Semantics, MergeSemantics, ExcludeSemantics)

        ◦ Primitive: The parallel SemanticsNode tree compiled from the RenderObject tree and exposed to Android TalkBack / iOS VoiceOver.

        ◦ Concept: Wrapping a compound card (e.g., Icon + Label + Value + Subtitle) in MergeSemantics combines fragmented child text nodes into a single coherent screen-reader announcement ("Today's Revenue: ₹42,500, up 12%") rather than forcing a blind user to swipe 4 separate times. Respect dynamic user font scaling
          (MediaQuery.textScalerOf(context)) up to 2.0x without clipping text inside ﬁxed-height SizedBox(height: 40) containers!

        ◦ Failure Mode: Building custom gesture cards with GestureDetector instead of InkWell or Semantics(button: true, label: ..., onTap: ...) and hardcoding ﬁxed heights on text containers, causing text clipping when a visually impaired user increases system font size.

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

   1. Q: Why does calling MediaQuery.of(context).size inside a dashboard screen cause severe frame drops when the soft keyboard opens on a child modal? A: MediaQuery.of(context) registers the widget as a listener to the entire MediaQuery InheritedWidget. As the keyboard slides up, viewInsets.bottom mutates on every
      animation frame, triggering a full rebuild of the dashboard even though size never changed. MediaQuery.sizeOf(context) uses InheritedModel to subscribe only to the size aspect.

   2. Q: What happens during a theme toggle animation if you forget to implement the lerp method properly in a custom ThemeExtension? A: Instead of smoothly interpolating colors and dimensions across the animation curve (t from 0.0 to 1.0), the theme tokens snap abruptly at the start or end of the transition.

   3. Q: Why should a card component never use SizedBox(height: 64) when it contains text labels? A: When a user sets OS accessibility text scaling to 1.5x or 2.0x, the text height grows beyond 64dp and either clips or throws a RenderFlex overﬂow. Always use ConstrainedBox(constraints: const BoxConstraints(minHeight:
      64)) with vertical padding so the card can grow with the font size.

   4. Q: What is the diﬀerence between using MediaQuery.sizeOf(context) and LayoutBuilder inside a reusable MetricCard widget? A: MediaQuery.sizeOf returns the entire app window width (e.g., 1200dp on tablet), whereas the MetricCard might be placed inside a narrow 300dp sidebar column. LayoutBuilder provides the exact
      BoxConstraints of the card's immediate parent slot.

   5. Q: What does MergeSemantics do in the Flutter Accessibility Tree? A: It merges the semantics of all descendant widgets into a single focusable node in the OS Accessibility Tree so screen readers read related labels as one cohesive sentence and maintain a clean swipe order.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe — Full Phase 1 Synthesis): Drive your Week 6 Adaptive UI Kit using a Week 3 sealed class LedgerViewState populated by Week 2 immutable Iterable analytics pipelines operating over Week 1 integer-safe currency primitives, styled strictly via Week 4 tokens and Week 5 keyed RenderFlex layouts.

Week 7: Ephemeral vs. Application State, Unidirectional Data Flow & Riverpod Foundations

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: UI is a pure, side-eﬀect-free mathematical function of immutable state: UI = f (State). State lives outside the ephemeral Widget tree inside an isolated Dependency Injection & Reactive Graph container (ProviderContainer / ProviderScope), where widgets subscribe to granular state slices via
     ref.watch and dispatch intent mutations upward via ref.read(provider.notifier).

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Phase 1 Comprehensive Mastery Gate (≥ 4/5 across Dart 3 sealed classes, value equality ==/hashCode, and the 3-Tree rendering pipeline).

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points (Primitive → Concept → Worked Example → Failure Mode)

   1. KP 1: Ephemeral (StatefulWidget) vs. Shared Application State (ProviderContainer Graph)

        ◦ Primitive: Local Element-bound memory vs. app-scoped Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) nodes inside ProviderScope.

        ◦ Concept: Keep purely visual, self-contained UI state (e.g., an animation controller, a text ﬁeld's cursor focus, or whether a single expansion tile is open) inside a StatefulWidget. Move domain data, cross-screen ﬁlters, and business logic into Riverpod Notifier<T> classes owned by ProviderScope so state survives widget unmounts
          and can be unit-tested in pure Dart without rendering widgets.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          @immutable
          class CartFilterState {
            final String query;
            final bool onlyInStock;
            const CartFilterState({this.query = '', this.onlyInStock = false});

              CartFilterState copyWith({String? query, bool? onlyInStock}) =>
                  CartFilterState(
                     query: query ?? this.query,
                     onlyInStock: onlyInStock ?? this.onlyInStock,
                  );

              @override
              bool operator ==(Object other) =>
                  identical(this, other) ||
                  other is CartFilterState &&
                      query == other.query &&
                      onlyInStock == other.onlyInStock;

              @override
              int get hashCode => Object.hash(query, onlyInStock);
          }

          class CartFilterNotifier extends Notifier<CartFilterState> {
            @override
              CartFilterState build() => const CartFilterState();

              void setQuery(String q) => state = state.copyWith(query: q.trim());
              void toggleStock() => state = state.copyWith(onlyInStock: !state.onlyInStock);
          }

          final cartFilterProvider =
              NotifierProvider<CartFilterNotifier, CartFilterState>(CartFilterNotifier.new);

        ◦ Failure Mode: Mutating properties on the current state object in place (state.items.add(newItem); state = state;). Because Riverpod compares oldState != newState to notify listeners, mutating the same heap reference in place results in zero UI updates.

  2. KP 2: Reactive Subscription Mechanics (ref.watch vs. ref.read vs. ref.listen & .select())

        ◦ Primitive: Observer registration on a ProviderElement linked to a Flutter ConsumerElement.

        ◦ Concept:

              ▪ ref.watch(provider): Subscribes the widget or computed provider to rebuild whenever the state changes. Use only inside build() methods.

              ▪ ref.watch(provider.select((s) => s.query)): Subscribes to a speciﬁc scalar property so changes to other ﬁelds on the state object do not trigger a widget rebuild.

              ▪ ref.read(provider.notifier): Reads the notiﬁer without subscribing. Use only inside gesture callbacks (onPressed, onSubmitted).

              ▪ ref.listen(provider, (prev, next) { ... }): Runs imperative side eﬀects (showing a SnackBar, navigating, triggering Haptics) when state transitions.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Calling ref.watch inside an onPressed callback (causing assertion crashes) or calling ref.read inside build() (causing the UI to display stale data that never updates when state changes).

  3. KP 3: Lifecycle Management (autoDispose), Family Parameters & Computed Providers

        ◦ Primitive: Reference-counted garbage collection of provider state nodes when active listeners drop to zero (onDispose).

        ◦ Concept: Use NotifierProvider.autoDispose for screen-scoped state so memory is freed automatically when the user pops the route, and combine multiple providers into derived, cached Computed Providers (Provider.autoDispose<List<Item>>) rather than duplicating derived state inside a Notiﬁer.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Storing derived/ﬁltered lists as a separate mutable ﬁeld inside a Notifier instead of computing them reactively in a dependent Provider, causing the ﬁltered list and source list to fall out of sync.

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why is synchronizing derived state inside a Notiﬁer method (e.g., manually updating state.totalPrice every time state.items changes) an architectural anti-pattern? A: Every manual mutation point risks forgetting to update the derived ﬁeld, creating state desynchronization bugs. Computing totalPrice inside a derived getter or a
     separate computed Provider that ref.watches the items list guarantees mathematical consistency.

  2. Q: What happens if you call ref.read(myProvider) inside a widget's build method? A: The widget reads the initial value once but never registers a subscription on the ProviderElement. Future state mutations will not rebuild the widget, leaving the UI stale.

  3. Q: How does ref.watch(userProvider.select((u) => u.avatarUrl)) optimize rendering performance? A: Riverpod evaluates the selector function on every userProvider emission and only triggers a rebuild of the listening Element if the selected return value (avatarUrl) changed according to ==.

  4. Q: Why should you trigger a SnackBar or dialog inside ref.listen rather than inside the build() method? A: build() must be pure and can run 60–120 times per second during animations or layout changes. Triggering imperative side eﬀects inside build() causes duplicate dialogs or setState() or markNeedsBuild() called
     during build crashes.

  5. Q: How do you test a Riverpod Notifier without pumping any Flutter widgets? A: Instantiate a pure Dart final container = ProviderContainer(); addTearDown(container.dispose);, invoke methods on container.read(myProvider.notifier), and assert on container.read(myProvider).

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Embed your Week 7 Riverpod state graph inside your Week 6 responsive AdaptiveScaffold (Compact Phone vs. Expanded Tablet split-view) so selecting an invoice on the left pane reactively updates the right inspector pane via a selectedInvoiceIdProvider while preserving scroll position.

Week 8: Asynchronous Reactive State (AsyncNotifier), Stream Providers, Cancellation & Debouncing

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Asynchronous operations are time-varying state machines that can overlap, fail, or outlive the screen that requested them. AsyncValue<T> (AsyncData, AsyncLoading, AsyncError) uniﬁes loading, error, and previous-data retention during refreshes, while cancellation tokens and debouncers prevent
     stale out-of-order network responses (race conditions) from overwriting newer state.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Week 7 Mastery Gate (≥ 4/5 on Synchronous Riverpod Notifier & UDF) + Week 3 Dart Future/Stream Event Loop mechanics.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: AsyncNotifier<T> & Exhaustive AsyncValue<T> State Transitions

        ◦ Primitive: A triad container holding value (T?), error (Object?, StackTrace?), and isLoading (bool) simultaneously.

        ◦ Concept: Unlike naive state classes that wipe out existing data when a refresh starts, AsyncValue<T> preserves the previous value while isLoading: true (or isRefreshing: true). Using .when(data: ..., error: ..., loading: ...) with skipLoadingOnRefresh: false or pattern matching on AsyncValue allows you to
          show a subtle top progress indicator during pull-to-refresh while keeping existing list items visible.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          class MarketCatalogNotifier extends AutoDisposeAsyncNotifier<List<AssetItem>> {
            @override
            Future<List<AssetItem>> build() async {
              final query = ref.watch(searchQueryProvider);
              if (query.isNotEmpty) {
                // Debounce rapid keystrokes by 350ms before firing request
                var disposed = false;
                ref.onDispose(() => disposed = true);
                await Future<void>.delayed(const Duration(milliseconds: 350));
                if (disposed) throw StateError('Cancelled during debounce');
              }
              return ref.read(catalogRepositoryProvider).fetchAssets(query: query);
            }

              Future<void> refreshCatalog() async {
                state = const AsyncLoading(); // Keeps previous data attached!
                state = await AsyncValue.guard(
                   () => ref.read(catalogRepositoryProvider).fetchAssets(
                          query: ref.read(searchQueryProvider),
                       ),
                );
              }
          }

        ◦ Failure Mode: Using try { state = AsyncData(await fetch()); } catch (e) { ... } manually and forgetting the StackTrace, or failing to guard against provider disposal mid-ﬂight (AsyncValue.guard captures both error and stack trace safely).

  2. KP 2: Eliminating Async Race Conditions, Debouncing & Request Cancellation

        ◦ Primitive: Out-of-order network packet arrival (t1 request "fl" takes 800ms; t2 request "flutter" takes 150ms → t1 resolves after t2 and overwrites the screen with stale "fl" results).

        ◦ Concept: Tie every async build() cycle to ref.onDispose. When a watched dependency (searchQueryProvider) changes, Riverpod immediately disposes the in-ﬂight execution cycle, ﬁring ref.onDispose callbacks to abort the pending timer or network token before starting the new build.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Firing independent async methods from onChanged callbacks in a TextField without cancelling the previous in-ﬂight request, causing ﬂickering stale search results.

  3. KP 3: Cursor/Oﬀset Pagination, Inﬁnite Scrolling & Optimistic Rollbacks

        ◦ Primitive: Windowed data slices (page, pageSize, hasMore, isFetchingNextPage) and optimistic state snapshots.

        ◦ Concept: For mutations (e.g., toggling a bookmark or archiving an invoice), capture final previousState = state;, immediately update state = AsyncData(optimisticList) for zero-latency UI feedback, await the remote call, and roll back to previousState inside catch if the server rejects the mutation.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Replacing the entire list state with const AsyncLoading() when fetching Page 2 of an inﬁnite scroll list, which destroys the ListView, resets the user's scroll oﬀset back to pixel 0.0, and ﬂashes a full-screen spinner.

   • Oﬃcial Docs to Read (riverpod.dev): Essentials → Performing side eﬀects; Canceling/Debouncing requests; Eager initialization; Pull to refresh; Oﬄine persistence / Optimistic updates; API Reference: AsyncValue & AsyncNotifier.

   • 30-Min Book Reading Assignment: A Common-Sense Guide to Data Structures and Algorithms (Jay Wengrow) — Chapters 1–4 (Why Data Structures Matter, Big-O Notation, and Speeding Up Your Code with Big-O). Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Understand why an O(N 2 ) nested loop inside a search ﬁlter or list diﬀ freezes a 120Hz mobile UI
     thread once N > 500."

   • Physical Notebook & Active Generation Drills (Paper Only):

        ◦ Draw a Timeline Sequence Diagram on paper illustrating an autocomplete race condition between Request A ("re", 600ms latency) and Request B ("react", 100ms latency), and draw how ref.onDispose + a cancellation ﬂag aborts Request A at t = 80ms.

        ◦ Handwrite the state transition table for an Inﬁnite Scroll Paginated Feed (InitialLoading, Loaded(items, hasMore: true), LoadingNextPage(existingItems), NextPageError(existingItems, error), Refreshing(existingItems)).

   • Obsidian /raw/ Capture & Mobile AI Sparring:

        ◦ Run Prompt 1 (Socratic Tutor): "Interrogate my understanding of how AsyncValue stores previous data during a transition to AsyncLoading, and quiz me on what happens if an AsyncNotifier throws an exception inside build() vs. inside a mutation method."

Active PC Protocol (Modular Tiers)

   • Tier A — Baseline (1 hr/day, Mon–Sat):

        ◦ Day 1: Build a simulated ﬂaky network repository (conﬁgurable latency 100–1200ms and 25% random failure rate) and consume it via AutoDisposeAsyncNotifier.

        ◦ Day 5–6: Build Weekly Deliverable: A Paginated, Debounced Product & Customer Search Explorer supporting inﬁnite scroll pagination (ScrollNotification threshold at 80% maxScrollExtent), pull-to-refresh, and optimistic favorite toggling.

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why does AsyncValue<T> allow both hasValue == true and isLoading == true at the exact same time? A: To support seamless UI refreshes and pagination where the screen keeps rendering the last known good data (value) while a background refresh or mutation is in ﬂight (isRefreshing == true).

  2. Q: What is the diﬀerence between ref.invalidate(myProvider) and ref.refresh(myProvider)? A: ref.invalidate marks the provider as dirty so it rebuilds on the next frame (if watched) or lazily on next read; ref.refresh forces an immediate synchronous rebuild and returns the new value/Future right away (ideal for
     RefreshIndicator.onRefresh: () => ref.refresh(myProvider.future)).

  3. Q: How does an async race condition occur in a search box even when using async/await? A: Each keystroke invokes a separate asynchronous execution context on the Event Loop. If Keystroke 1 takes 500ms over the network and Keystroke 2 takes 100ms, Keystroke 2 completes ﬁrst, and 400ms later Keystroke 1 resolves and overwrites the
     state with outdated results.

  4. Q: What are the 4 mandatory steps of an Optimistic UI mutation with rollback? A: (1) Snapshot final previous = state;, (2) Immediately emit state = AsyncData(optimisticData);, (3) try { await api.mutate(); }, (4) catch (e, st) { state = previous; /* notify user */ }.

  5. Q: Why does checking index == items.length - 1 inside ListView.builder or listening for ScrollMetrics.pixels >= ScrollMetrics.maxScrollExtent * 0.8 need an isFetchingNextPage guard? A: Because a single swipe gesture ﬁres dozens of scroll events per second; without a synchronous boolean/state lock (if
     (state.isFetchingMore || !state.hasMore) return;), you will ﬁre 20 duplicate requests for Page 2 simultaneously.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Ensure your Week 8 Paginated Explorer uses Week 5 ValueKey(item.id) on every list tile, Week 6 MergeSemantics for screen readers announcing loading/error states, and Week 4 skeleton shimmer placeholders.

        1. Debounced search input (300ms) that cancels in-ﬂight searches on new keystrokes.

        2. Pull-to-refresh that retains existing list items on screen while refreshing (RefreshIndicator + .future).

        3. Optimistic "Mark as Fulﬁlled" toggle on each order row that instantaneously updates the UI badge and rolls back cleanly with an error SnackBar if the mock repository throws an exception.

Week 9: Declarative Routing (GoRouter), Deep Linking, Nested Navigation Shells & Auth Guards

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Navigation is not an imperative stack of ad-hoc .push() calls; it is a declarative state graph where the current URI location (/orders/42?tab=items) is a serializable source of truth that deterministically constructs the active stack of Pages, nested Navigator shells, and route-level authorization
     redirects.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–8 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: Imperative Navigator 1.0 Stack vs. Declarative Router / GoRouter URI Graph

        ◦ Primitive: RouteInformationParser ↔ RouterDelegate ↔ Navigator.pages list reconciliation.

        ◦ Concept: In declarative routing (go_router), context.go('/orders/42') declarative sets the target URI path (reconciling the ancestor route hierarchy deﬁned in the route tree), whereas context.push('/modal') imperatively pushes a route on top of the current navigation stack. Path parameters (:id) and query parameters (?
          filter=paid) make every screen deep-linkable from push notiﬁcations, web URLs, or OS App Links.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          GoRoute(
            path: '/orders',
            builder: (context, state) => const OrdersListScreen(),
            routes: [
               GoRoute(
                  path: ':orderId', // Matches /orders/104
                  builder: (context, state) {
                     final id = state.pathParameters['orderId']!;
                     final tab = state.uri.queryParameters['tab'] ?? 'summary';
                     return OrderDetailScreen(orderId: id, initialTab: tab);
                  },
               ),
            ],
          )

        ◦ Failure Mode: Passing complex mutable Dart objects via extra (context.go('/details', extra: myHeavyObject)) for primary screens. If the OS kills and restores the backgrounded app or the user opens a deep link, extra is null and the screen crashes! Always pass canonical IDs via pathParameters and read the entity from
          Riverpod/cache.

  2. KP 2: Stateful Nested Navigation via StatefulShellRoute.indexedStack

        ◦ Primitive: Root Navigator vs. Nested Branch Navigators backed by an IndexedStack container.

        ◦ Concept: Bottom navigation bars and tablet navigation rails require each tab branch (e.g., Tab 0: /dashboard, Tab 1: /inventory/item/5, Tab 2: /settings) to maintain its own independent navigation history and scroll position when switching tabs. StatefulShellRoute.indexedStack provides a StatefulNavigationShell that
          switches active branches without disposing inactive tab trees.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Placing a new Scaffold with a fresh NavigationBar inside every individual screen instead of using a single StatefulShellRoute, causing the bottom bar to animate/slide away on every sub-route push and losing scroll position on tab switches.

  3. KP 3: Reactive Route Guards (redirect) & Deep-Link Destination Preservation

        ◦ Primitive: Synchronous/Asynchronous route interceptor (BuildContext, GoRouterState) -> FutureOr<String?> triggered by a refreshListenable stream/notiﬁer.

        ◦ Concept: Bridge Riverpod's authStateProvider to GoRouter's refreshListenable (via a ValueNotifier or ChangeNotifier adapter listening to ref.listen). When an unauthenticated user clicks a deep link to /orders/42, the redirect guard intercepts the navigation, redirects to /login?from=%2Forders%2F42, and
          automatically routes them to /orders/42 the instant login succeeds.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Recreating the entire GoRouter(...) instance inside a Provider every time ref.watch(authStateProvider) changes! Recreating GoRouter wipes out the entire navigation stack and resets the app to /. Instead, construct GoRouter once and trigger re-evaluation via refreshListenable.

Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Every navigation branch is a LIFO Stack of Route pages, while deep-link URI matching traverses a tree of path segments."

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: If the user is currently at /home and you execute context.go('/orders/42') (where :orderId is a child route under /orders), what is the resulting navigation stack when the user presses the system Back button? A: The stack is constructed declaratively from the route hierarchy: ['/home' (or root), '/orders', '/orders/42'].
     Pressing Back pops /orders/42 and lands on /orders, even though the user never manually visited /orders ﬁrst.

  2. Q: Why must a Provider<GoRouter> NEVER call ref.watch(authProvider) directly if authProvider emits multiple states during app usage? A: Calling ref.watch causes the Provider<GoRouter> to rebuild and return a brand-new GoRouter instance on every auth state change, which destroys the RouterDelegate and resets the
     user's navigation stack. Instead, use ref.listen inside the provider to notify a ValueNotifier passed to refreshListenable.

  3. Q: How do you make a route deﬁned inside a StatefulShellBranch open full-screen over the bottom NavigationBar instead of inside the tab's body? A: Pass parentNavigatorKey: rootNavigatorKey to that speciﬁc GoRoute, instructing GoRouter to push that page onto the top-level root Navigator above the StatefulShellRoute.

  4. Q: Why is passing a domain object via state.extra dangerous for deep-linkable routes? A: extra lives only in ephemeral RAM and cannot be serialized into a URI string. If a user opens the route via a cold-start deep link or OS state restoration, state.extra will be null.

  5. Q: How do you prevent a user from accidentally pressing the Android system Back button and losing an unsaved 10-ﬁeld invoice form? A: Wrap the form screen in PopScope(canPop: !hasUnsavedChanges, onPopInvokedWithResult: (didPop, result) async { if (!didPop) { /* show confirmation dialog */ } }) or
     use GoRoute.onExit.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Synchronize your Week 8 debounced search and ﬁlter state with GoRouter query parameters (/inventory?q=bolt&inStock=true) so sharing or bookmarking the URI restores the exact ﬁltered AsyncNotifier query.

Week 10: Online Networking (Dio), Interceptors, Resilient Retry Pipelines & Functional Error Boundaries

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: The network is an untrusted, high-latency I/O boundary that will drop packets, return malformed schemas, and throttle requests. Raw HTTP status codes and socket exceptions must never leak into the UI layer; they must be intercepted, retried idempotently with exponential backoﬀ + jitter, validated
     through strict DTO-to-Domain mappers, and wrapped into typed AppFailure contracts.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–9 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: HTTP/2 Lifecycle, Dio Client Architecture & Request/Response/Error Interceptors

        ◦ Primitive: TCP/TLS socket pooling, HTTP headers/body streams, and middleware interceptor chains (onRequest → Network → onResponse / onError).

        ◦ Concept: Conﬁgure a singleton Dio client via a Riverpod Provider<Dio> with explicit connectTimeout, receiveTimeout, and sendTimeout. Attach modular Interceptor classes for structured telemetry logging, correlation ID injection (X-Request-ID), and uniﬁed error translation.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          sealed class AppFailure {
            final String userMessage;
            const AppFailure(this.userMessage);
          }
          final class NetworkOfflineFailure extends AppFailure {
            const NetworkOfflineFailure() : super('No internet connection. Check your network.');
          }
          final class ServerTimeoutFailure extends AppFailure {
            const ServerTimeoutFailure() : super('Server took too long to respond.');
          }
          final class ApiHttpFailure extends AppFailure {
            final int statusCode;
            const ApiHttpFailure(this.statusCode, super.userMessage);
          }
          final class SchemaParseFailure extends AppFailure {
            const SchemaParseFailure() : super('Received unexpected data format from server.');
          }

        ◦ Failure Mode: Leaving Dio timeouts at default (inﬁnite or excessively long) or catching raw DioException inside a Widget build/onPressed method instead of mapping it at the Repository boundary into a domain-speciﬁc AppFailure.

  2. KP 2: Resilient Retry with Exponential Backoﬀ + Jitter & CancelToken Lifecycle

        ◦ Primitive: Transient network errors (HTTP 408, 429 Too Many Requests, 502, 503, 504, DioExceptionType.connectionTimeout) vs. Non-retryable client errors (400, 401, 403, 404, 422).

        ◦ Concept: Only retry idempotent requests (GET, PUT, DELETE, or POST with an Idempotency-Key header) on transient 5xx/timeout errors using exponential backoﬀ with random jitter: tdelay = base × 2attempt + random(0, 300ms) to prevent "thundering herd" server overload. Bind a Dio CancelToken to Riverpod's
          ref.onDispose(cancelToken.cancel) so leaving a screen immediately aborts the underlying HTTP socket.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Blindly retrying a non-idempotent POST /payments request without an Idempotency-Key when a response times out, charging the customer's credit card 3 times!

  3. KP 3: Defensive DTO Parsing (Data Transfer Objects) vs. Domain Entities

        ◦ Primitive: Untrusted Map<String, dynamic> JSON trees from external APIs.

        ◦ Concept: Never use raw JSON maps in your UI or state layer, and never let a single unexpected null ﬁeld in 1 of 50 list items crash the entire screen with type 'Null' is not a subtype of type 'String' in type cast. Separate DTOs (OrderDto.fromJson using defensive pattern matching or json_serializable/freezed
          with default fallbacks) from clean Domain Models (Order with integer currency minor units and non-nullable invariants).

        ◦ Failure Mode: Using unsafe json['price'] as double casts directly inside domain models, causing a single backend API ﬁeld rename or null value to brick the mobile app in production.

   • 30-Min Book Reading Assignment: A Common-Sense Guide to Data Structures and Algorithms — Chapters 10–11 (Recursively Recurse with Recursion, and Learning to Write in Recursive — applied to nested JSON AST parsing and backoﬀ trees). Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Every external payload is a tree of untrusted nodes; validate structural
     invariants at the boundary before admitting data into your domain."

   • Physical Notebook & Active Generation Drills (Paper Only):

        ◦ Draw the complete Request-to-UI Pipeline on paper: ConsumerWidget → AsyncNotifier (creates CancelToken bound to ref.onDispose) → OrderRepository (catches DioException & FormatException, maps OrderDto → Order, returns Result<List<Order>, AppFailure>) → RetryInterceptor → Dio HTTP Client.

        ◦ Handwrite a custom RetryInterceptor extends Interceptor in your notebook that inspects err.type and err.response?.statusCode, waits 2attempt × 500ms, and re-dispatches the request via dio.fetch(err.requestOptions) up to 3 times.

   • Obsidian /raw/ Capture & Mobile AI Sparring:

        ◦ Run Prompt 1 (Socratic Tutor): "Challenge my mental model on why we add random jitter to exponential backoﬀ, which HTTP methods and status codes are safe to auto-retry without an Idempotency-Key, and why DTOs should be separate from Domain Entities."

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why must a RetryInterceptor NOT automatically retry an HTTP 400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized, or 422 Unprocessable Entity? A: Client 4xx errors (except 408 Request Timeout and 429 Too Many Requests) indicate deterministic validation or authentication failures; resending the exact same payload will fail identically
     and waste bandwidth/battery.

  2. Q: What happens on the server if 10,000 mobile clients lose connection during a 2-second server blip and all retry at exact intervals of 1.0s, 2.0s, 4.0s without jitter? A: They create synchronized traﬃc spikes ("thundering herd") that repeatedly knock the recovering server oﬄine. Adding random jitter spreads the retry load evenly across time.

  3. Q: How does binding Dio's CancelToken to ref.onDispose in an autoDispose provider protect mobile performance? A: When the user navigates back from a screen before a slow HTTP response ﬁnishes, onDispose invokes cancelToken.cancel(), closing the network stream immediately and skipping unnecessary JSON deserialization.

  4. Q: Why should UI widgets never catch DioException directly? A: Leaky abstraction: coupling the Presentation layer to a speciﬁc networking package (Dio) makes switching data sources (e.g., to local SQLite in Phase 3) or testing UI states impossible without mocking low-level HTTP objects.

  5. Q: How do you prevent a single malformed item in a JSON list of 100 items from breaking the entire API call? A: Iterate through the raw JSON List<dynamic>, wrap each item's DTO parsing inside an individual try/catch block, log schema defects for corrupt items, and return the valid List<DomainEntity>.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Wire your Week 10 Dio repository into your Week 8 AsyncNotifier and Week 9 GoRouter deep-linkable detail view (/suppliers/:id) so navigating back mid-load aborts the CancelToken cleanly.
