     SemanticsDebugger).

Week 21: Choreographed Micro-Interactions, Physics Simulations (SpringSimulation) & Vector State Machines (Rive)

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Motion in high-craft UI is never decorative ﬂourish; it communicates spatial continuity (where an element came from and where it went), causal feedback (conﬁrming gesture velocity and state change), and system status. True physical responsiveness requires replacing ﬁxed-duration linear/Bezier
     timers with continuous velocity-preserving spring physics (F = −kx − cv via SpringSimulation), so interrupting a gesture mid-ﬂight redirects momentum smoothly without jarring velocity discontinuities.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–20 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: The Hardware VSYNC Ticker Loop, Implicit (TweenAnimationBuilder) vs. Explicit (AnimationController) Animations

        ◦ Primitive: SchedulerBinding.scheduleFrameCallback driven by the display's 60Hz/120Hz hardware vertical sync (VSYNC) interrupt via Ticker.

        ◦ Concept: A Ticker ﬁres a callback on every hardware display refresh, passing the elapsed Duration since start.

             ▪ Implicit Animations (AnimatedContainer, AnimatedSwitcher, TweenAnimationBuilder): Fire-and-forget target value transitions managed automatically by the framework.

             ▪ Explicit Animations (AnimationController + SingleTickerProviderStateMixin): Imperative lifecycle control (forward(), reverse(), repeat(), animateWith(Simulation)), staggered interval sequencing (Interval(0.2, 0.6, curve: Curves.easeOutCubic)), and direct binding to AnimatedBuilder /
               ValueListenableBuilder or RenderObject properties.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          // Staggered choreographed entrance using a single AnimationController
          late final _controller = AnimationController(
             vsync: this,
             duration: const Duration(milliseconds: 500),
          );
          late final _headerSlide = Tween<Offset>(
             begin: const Offset(0, 0.15),
             end: Offset.zero,
          ).animate(CurvedAnimation(
             parent: _controller,
             curve: const Interval(0.0, 0.6, curve: Curves.easeOutCubic),
          ));

        ◦ Failure Mode: Forgetting to call _controller.dispose() in State.dispose() (causing a Ticker leak that throws an active ticker assertion and drains battery forever) or forgetting TickerMode / SingleTickerProviderStateMixin.

  2. KP 2: Physics-Based Motion (SpringSimulation) vs. Fixed-Duration Bezier Curves

        ◦ Primitive: Damped harmonic oscillator diﬀerential equation governed by SpringDescription(mass: m, stiffness: k, damping: c) and initial gesture velocity v0 (details.velocity.pixelsPerSecond).

        ◦ Concept: Fixed-duration Curves.easeInOut animations ignore how fast the user ﬂicked their ﬁnger and look robotic if interrupted mid-ﬂight. When a user drags a bottom sheet or swipeable card and releases (onPanEnd), feeding the ﬁnger's real release velocity (unitsPerSecond) into
          _controller.animateWith(SpringSimulation(spring, currentValue, targetValue, unitVelocity)) preserves physical momentum seamlessly.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Using under-damped "bouncy" springs (dampingRatio < 0.7) on serious enterprise/ﬁnancial UI tables (causing nauseating oscillation), or ignoring MediaQuery.disableAnimationsOf(context) for users with vestibular motion disorders!

  3. KP 3: Shared-Element Spatial Transitions (Hero) & Interactive Vector State Machines (Rive)

        ◦ Primitive: HeroController overlay ﬂight shuttle interpolation between source and destination RenderBox global bounds, and Rive SMIInput<bool/number> state-machine inputs.

        ◦ Concept: Use Hero (or Material 3 OpenContainer container transform) to maintain spatial orientation when expanding a compact list card into a full detail screen. Use Rive state machines for interactive vector components (e.g., an animated sync status icon that transitions smoothly from Idle → SyncingLoop → SuccessCheckmark /
          ConflictWarning driven directly by Riverpod's SyncStatus enum).

        ◦ Failure Mode: Assigning duplicate Hero.tag values to two visible items in the same route (crashing the route transition with There are multiple heroes that share the same tag within a subtree).

   • 30-Min Book Reading Assignment: Designing Data-Intensive Applications — Chapter 3 (Storage Engines: Log-Structured LSM-Trees vs. Page-Oriented B-Trees, Write-Ahead Logs, and Compaction). Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Understand why append-only logs (LSM-Trees) optimize write throughput while B-Trees optimize predictable read latency
     —directly mirroring how our Week 15 sync_outbox log feeds our indexed Drift B-Tree tables."

   • Physical Notebook & Active Generation Drills (Paper Only):

        ◦ Draw a Choreographed Timeline Graph (t = 0ms → 450ms) for an "Invoice Paid Conﬁrmation" ﬂow: trace the overlapping Interval windows for (1) Card elevation scale, (2) Rive checkmark state machine trigger, (3) Staggered line-item receipt reveal, and (4) Haptic feedback pulse.

        ◦ Write down the math converting DragEndDetails.velocity.pixelsPerSecond.dy into normalized controller velocity (pixelsPerSecond.dy / maxDragDistance) to initialize a SpringSimulation.

   • Obsidian /raw/ Capture & Mobile AI Sparring:

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why do physics-based SpringSimulation animations feel noticeably more natural on touchscreens than animateTo(duration: 300ms, curve: Curves.easeOut)? A: Because a ﬁxed-duration curve starts from velocity 0 regardless of how fast the user ﬂicked their ﬁnger, creating a jarring kinetic break at the moment of ﬁnger
     release. SpringSimulation accepts the ﬁnger's exact release velocity v0 and smoothly decelerates along a continuous physical trajectory.

  2. Q: Where does a Hero widget actually live in the widget tree while it is ﬂying between Route A and Route B? A: During the transition ﬂight, the HeroController removes the widget from both routes (replacing them with empty SizedBox placeholders of the same size) and mounts the ﬂying shuttle widget inside the top-level Navigator's
     Overlay above both routes.

  3. Q: Why does an ElevatedButton or Material text inside a ﬂying Hero sometimes ﬂash giant yellow double-underlined text during route transitions? A: Because the Overlay sits above the route's Scaffold (Material ancestor). When the Hero widget is lifted into the Overlay mid-ﬂight, it loses its Material ancestor unless wrapped in a
     Material(type: MaterialType.transparency, child: ...).

  4. Q: How do you ensure your app does not trigger motion sickness for users who enabled "Reduce Motion / Remove Animations" in iOS or Android accessibility settings? A: Check MediaQuery.disableAnimationsOf(context): when true, bypass decorative slide/scale/spring transitions (or set duration to Duration.zero / simple cross-
     fade) while keeping functional state updates intact.

  5. Q: Why is animating Transform.translate or SlideTransition drastically cheaper than animating Padding or SizedBox(height: ...)? A: Animating Padding or SizedBox marks the RenderObject dirty for layout (markNeedsLayout) on every single 120Hz frame, forcing parent/sibling relayout cascades. Transform operates strictly
     during the paint/compositing phase via a GPU matrix multiplication without touching layout.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Connect your Week 15 OutboxSyncEngine state transitions and Week 20 CustomPainter charts to Week 21 physics-driven bottom sheets and Rive state-machine indicators.

(45 Mins — IDE Execution): Build an Interactive Swipeable Approval Card & Physics Drawer where cards can be dragged in 2D, caught mid-ﬂight during return spring motion, ﬂicked oﬀ-screen when velocity or displacement exceeds a threshold (triggering a staggered receipt reveal), and cleanly snaps with zero motion when
           MediaQuery.disableAnimationsOf(context) is true.

Week 22: The Automated Veriﬁcation Pyramid: Unit, Widget, Golden Pixel-Diﬀ & E2E Integration Testing

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Untested code is a liability that decays under refactoring; production engineering requires a deterministic Veriﬁcation Pyramid:

        1. Base (70% — Pure Dart Unit Tests): Fast (< 1ms/test) veriﬁcation of Domain entities, Drift in-memory DAOs, OutboxSyncEngine conﬂict mergers, and Riverpod ProviderContainer state machines using fakes/mocks (mocktail).

        2. Middle (20% — Widget & Golden Tests): Headless frame-pumping (WidgetTester.pumpWidget) verifying UI state transitions, accessibility semantics (matchesSemantics), and pixel-perfect visual regression (Golden Tests).

        3. Peak (10% — Integration Tests): End-to-end user journeys across screens, routing, and persistence.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–21 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: Pure Dart State & Repository Testing (ProviderContainer, mocktail, & In-Memory Drift)

        ◦ Primitive: Dependency inversion via ProviderScope(overrides: [...]) and ProviderContainer listener spies.

        ◦ Concept: Because Riverpod decouples state from the widget tree and Drift can run against NativeDatabase.memory(), you can test 100% of your database queries, 409 conﬂict resolution, and queued 401 token refresh logic in milliseconds without launching a simulator. Use Fakes (lightweight working in-memory implementations) or
          mocktail (when(() => ...).thenAnswer(...), verify(...)) strictly at external I/O boundaries (like the HTTP adapter), never mocking internal domain models.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          test('OutboxSyncEngine resolves non-overlapping 409 conflict via 3-way merge', () async {
            final db = AppDatabase(NativeDatabase.memory());
            addTearDown(db.close);
            final mockRemote = MockOrdersRemoteDataSource();
            final container = ProviderContainer(
               overrides: [
                  appDatabaseProvider.overrideWithValue(db),
                  ordersRemoteProvider.overrideWithValue(mockRemote),
               ],
            );
            addTearDown(container.dispose);
            // Arrange base state, offline edit, and mock 409 server response -> Act -> Assert merged row in db
          });

        ◦ Failure Mode: Over-mocking internal classes so tests break every time you refactor a private helper method, while failing to test actual SQL constraints in NativeDatabase.memory().

  2. KP 2: Headless Widget Testing (pump vs. pumpAndSettle), FakeAsync & Accessibility Audits

        ◦ Primitive: Virtual clock frame stepping (tester.pump(Duration)) inside the TestWidgetsFlutterBinding environment.

        ◦ Concept: In widget tests, frames do not advance automatically. await tester.pump() triggers exactly one build/layout/paint frame; await tester.pumpAndSettle() repeatedly pumps frames until no more animations or microtasks are scheduled. Furthermore, running await expectLater(tester,
          meetsGuideline(textContrastGuideline)) and meetsGuideline(androidTapTargetGuideline) automatically audits your widget tree for WCAG contrast and 48 × 48dp touch-target violations!

        ◦ Failure Mode: Calling await tester.pumpAndSettle() on a screen that displays an inﬁnite looping animation (e.g., an indeterminate CircularProgressIndicator, skeleton shimmer, or looping Rive animation)—causing pumpAndSettle to loop until it crashes with pumpAndSettle timed out! Always use await
          tester.pump(const Duration(milliseconds: 100)) when testing loading states.

  3. KP 3: Golden Visual Regression Testing (matchesGoldenFile) & Deterministic Font/Asset Loading

        ◦ Primitive: Headless software rasterization of a widget subtree into a PNG buﬀer compared pixel-by-pixel against a committed baseline snapshot.

        ◦ Concept: Golden tests catch accidental visual regressions (e.g., a token change or constraint bug shifting a price label by 4px or breaking Dark Mode contrast) across multiple simulated screen sizes (Phone 375x812, Tablet 1024x768) and text scales (1.0x, 2.0x).

        ◦ Failure Mode: Running Golden tests without loading your app's bundled .ttf fonts and Material icons in flutter_test_config.dart, which causes Flutter's test runner to render illegible solid square blocks (Ahem debug font) instead of real typography.

Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Every mobile app with an oﬄine SQLite cache is a multi-leader replication

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: How does testWidgets execute a 500ms debounce timer or a 10-second network timeout test in < 5ms of real time? A: testWidgets runs inside a FakeAsync zone that intercepts Timer and Future.delayed creation. Calling await tester.pump(const Duration(seconds: 10)) advances the virtual clock instantaneously without
     sleeping the OS thread.

  2. Q: Why does await tester.pumpAndSettle() fail with a timeout when testing a screen that has an active skeleton shimmer or spinning progress indicator? A: pumpAndSettle repeatedly advances frames until SchedulerBinding.hasScheduledFrame is false. Because an inﬁnite animation schedules a new frame on every tick, the loop
     never terminates and hits the 10-minute virtual timeout.

  3. Q: Why must you always register addTearDown(container.dispose) when testing Riverpod providers with ProviderContainer? A: To ensure all active streams, timers, and onDispose hooks inside watched providers are cleaned up between tests and to verify that disposal itself does not throw exceptions.

  4. Q: What 3 built-in accessibility guidelines can expectLater(tester, meetsGuideline(...)) automatically verify in a Flutter widget test? A: (1) Minimum touch target size (androidTapTargetGuideline 48 × 48dp / iOSTapTargetGuideline 44 × 44pt), (2) Semantic labels on interactive controls (labeledTapTargetGuideline), and
     (3) WCAG text contrast ratios (textContrastGuideline).

  5. Q: Why is testing Drift DAOs against NativeDatabase.memory() vastly superior to mocking the DAO interface with mocktail? A: Mocking a DAO only tests that your Dart code called a method name; it completely misses broken SQL syntax, missing foreign-key constraints, wrong JOIN conditions, and migration bugs that
     NativeDatabase.memory() catches immediately against the real SQLite C engine.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Write a combined test suite that veriﬁes your Week 8 debounced search (FakeAsync), Week 11 queued 401 refresh interceptor, Week 15 Drift in-memory outbox sync, and Week 20 CustomPainter Golden snapshots.

(45 Mins — IDE Execution): Write (a) a pure Dart FakeAsync unit test proving the debounce + exponential backoﬀ logic, (b) an in-memory Drift transaction rollback test, and (c) a testWidgets test that pumps the UI through Loading (without pumpAndSettle timeout!) → Error → Retry Tap → Populated and passes all 3
           meetsGuideline accessibility assertions.

Week 23: Impeller Engine Proﬁling, DevTools Memory/Frame Audits, App Security & Automated CI/CD Pipelines

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: Production engineering means guaranteeing deterministic performance, memory stability, and repeatable delivery under real-world device constraints:

        1. Frame Budget Invariant: On a 120Hz display, both the UI Thread (Dart build/layout/paint recording) and the Raster Thread (Impeller GPU command buﬀer execution) must complete every frame in ≤ 8.33ms (≤ 16.67ms at 60Hz).

        2. Zero-Leak Heap Invariant: Retained heap memory must return to baseline when popping routes.

        3. Automated CI/CD Gate: No code merges to main unless an automated pipeline (GitHub Actions) veriﬁes formatting, strict static analysis, unit/widget/golden tests, and signed release binary compilation.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–22 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: The Two-Thread Rendering Pipeline (UI Thread vs. Raster Thread) & Impeller Engine Architecture
                                                                                                                                                             PipelineItem
        ◦ Primitive: UI Thread (Widget.build → Element → RenderObject.layout → RenderObject.paint producing a LayerTree / Display List)                                     Raster Thread (Impeller pre-compiled Metal/Vulkan tessellation & shaders → GPU frame buﬀer).

        ◦ Concept: Unlike the legacy Skia engine (which compiled shaders at runtime, causing ﬁrst-run "shader compilation jank"), Impeller pre-compiles a smaller, ﬁxed set of shaders at build time and uses explicit Metal (iOS) and Vulkan (Android) command queues. When proﬁling in Flutter DevTools Performance View (always in --profile
          mode on a real physical device, never --debug mode!):

             ▪ Tall UI Thread Bars (> 8.3ms): Caused by synchronous CPU work on the main Isolate (heavy JSON parsing, O(N 2 ) list ﬁltering, rebuilding 500 widgets unnecessarily, IntrinsicHeight in lists).

             ▪ Tall Raster Thread Bars (> 8.3ms): Caused by expensive GPU operations (unbounded saveLayer, BackdropFilter blurs inside scrolling lists, clipping complex paths without caching, or massive unscaled 4000x4000px images decoded into GPU texture memory without cacheWidth/cacheHeight).

        ◦ Worked Example:

          // Prevents decoding a 4000x3000 (48MB RAM) camera image when rendering a 64x64dp thumbnail!
          Image.file(
              receiptFile,
              width: 64,
              height: 64,
              cacheWidth: (64 * MediaQuery.devicePixelRatioOf(context)).round(),
              fit: BoxFit.cover,
          )

        ◦ Failure Mode: Proﬁling frame rates in --debug mode (which runs JIT unoptimized Dart code and assertions, distorting CPU metrics by 5–10×) or loading 20 full-resolution camera photos into a ListView without cacheWidth, triggering an instant Out-Of-Memory (OOM) crash on mid-range Android devices!

  2. KP 2: DevTools Memory Heap Snapshots, Diﬃng & Eliminating Retained Object Leaks

        ◦ Primitive: Dart VM Garbage Collector (Young Space Scavenger + Old Space Mark-Sweep) and Retaining Paths from GC Roots.

        ◦ Concept: The GC can only free objects that are unreachable from GC Roots. To hunt a memory leak in DevTools Memory View: (1) Take Heap Snapshot 1 on the Home screen, (2) Push the suspect Detail screen, interact with it, and Pop back to Home, (3) Force a Manual GC and take Heap Snapshot 2, (4) Diﬀ Snapshot 2 against Snapshot 1
          —if DetailScreenState, TextEditingController, AnimationController, or StreamSubscription instances still have count > 0, inspect their Retaining Path to see which global singleton or uncancelled closure is holding them hostage.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Passing a widget's BuildContext or this into a long-lived singleton callback without unregistering in dispose(), pinning the entire screen's Element and RenderObject subtree in Old Space RAM forever.

  3. KP 3: Production Hardening (--obfuscate, Env Flavors, Crash Telemetry) & GitHub Actions CI/CD

        ◦ Primitive: Compile-time environment injection (--dart-define-from-file), Dart AOT symbol stripping (--obfuscate --split-debug-info), Sentry crash symbolication, and declarative YAML CI runners.

        ◦ Concept: Separate dev, staging, and prod environments cleanly. Never commit API secrets or signing keystores (upload-keystore.jks) to Git; store them in encrypted GitHub Actions Secrets. Build release binaries (flutter build appbundle --release --obfuscate --split-debug-info=build/symbols) and upload debug
          symbols to Sentry / Crashlytics so obfuscated production stack traces de-symbolicate into readable ﬁle/line numbers.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Shipping an unobfuscated production APK with hardcoded staging keys or failing to upload --split-debug-info symbols—making production crash reports completely unreadable (_kDartIsolateSnapshotInstructions+0x4a2f)!

Passive Shop Protocol (2–4 hrs/day, Mon–Sat)

   • Oﬃcial Docs to Read (docs.flutter.dev): Perf → Performance best practices, Impeller rendering engine, Use the Performance view, Use the Memory view; Deployment → Obfuscating Dart code, Continuous delivery with Flutter (GitHub Actions), Build and release an Android app / iOS app.

   • 30-Min Book Reading Assignment: Designing Data-Intensive Applications — Chapter 11 (Stream Processing: Transmitting Event Streams, Databases and Streams, Change Data Capture, Event Sourcing, and Fault-Tolerant Stream Processing). Extract into /raw/book_notes/: "Every real-time mobile app is a stream processor consuming event
     streams (gestures, SQLite table diﬀs, WebSocket CDC) and materializing them into 120Hz display frames."

   • Physical Notebook & Active Generation Drills (Paper Only):

        ◦ Draw the Two-Thread Frame Pipeline Diagram (VSYNC → UI Thread [Build/Layout/Paint <= 8.3ms] → LayerTree → Raster Thread [Impeller GPU <= 8.3ms] → Display) and list 4 speciﬁc causes of UI-thread jank vs. 4 speciﬁc causes of Raster-thread jank on paper.

        ◦ Handwrite a complete .github/workflows/flutter_ci.yml pipeline from memory that runs on every Pull Request: (1) Checkout & cache Flutter SDK, (2) dart format --set-exit-if-changed ., (3) flutter analyze --fatal-infos, (4) flutter test --coverage, and (5) flutter build apk --release --obfuscate
          --split-debug-info=symbols/.

   • Obsidian /raw/ Capture & Mobile AI Sparring:

        ◦ Run Prompt 1 (Socratic Tutor): "Present me with 4 real-world Flutter DevTools ﬂame-chart and heap-snapshot scenarios (e.g., high Raster time with low UI time; Old Space memory climbing by 15MB on every route push/pop) and force me to diagnose the exact root cause and ﬁx."

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck

  1. Q: Why must you NEVER evaluate Flutter frame times or animations in --debug mode? A: --debug mode compiles Dart using an unoptimized JIT compiler (for hot reload) and executes hundreds of expensive framework assert() checks on every frame. Always proﬁle in --profile mode on a physical device, which compiles AOT native
     machine code identical to --release while keeping DevTools proﬁling hooks enabled.

  2. Q: In Flutter DevTools Performance View, if the UI Thread takes 1.8ms per frame but the Raster Thread takes 24.0ms per frame (dropping frames to 40fps), where is the bottleneck? A: The Dart code (build/layout/paint recording) is fast, but the Display List contains expensive GPU raster commands—such as BackdropFilter blurs,
     saveLayer alpha compositing, complex clipped shadows, or oversized textures without cacheWidth.

  3. Q: How much raw bitmap RAM does a single 4000 × 3000 pixel JPEG photo consume once decoded into RGBA GPU texture memory if cacheWidth is omitted? A: 4000 × 3000 × 4 bytes (RGBA) = 48, 000, 000 bytes ≈ 48 MB of RAM for a single image—even if displayed inside a tiny 60 × 60dp avatar circle!

  4. Q: What is the purpose of --split-debug-info=build/symbols when building with --obfuscate? A: --obfuscate renames your Dart class and method names into short random symbols (a, b, c) to shrink binary size and deter reverse-engineering. --split-debug-info exports the mapping table so you can upload it to Sentry/
     Crashlytics to translate obfuscated production crash traces back into human-readable stack traces.

  5. Q: What is a Retaining Path in a DevTools Memory Heap Snapshot? A: The exact chain of object references leading from a GC Root (such as a global variable, active Isolate port, or static singleton) down to the leaked object, showing precisely which reference is preventing the Garbage Collector from freeing it.

   • Interleaved Task (FIRe): Proﬁle your Week 19 50,000-row Sliver list, Week 20 120fps CustomPainter chart, and Week 18 Isolate sync batch in --profile mode on a physical phone while monitoring DevTools Frame Timing and Memory Heap.

Week 24: Commercial Product Delivery, RFC Scoping, Client Handover & Flagship Capstone Launch (v6.0.0)

   • Core First-Principles Mental Model: A principal engineer and product designer does not merely write code; they close the entire commercial loop: \text{Discover (Pain Point)} \rightarrow \text{Scope (RFC/SOW)} \rightarrow \text{Design (Figma Tokens & Prototype)} \rightarrow \text{Architect (ERD, RLS, Oﬄine Sync)} \rightarrow \text{Verify
     (90\%+ Tests & Impeller Proﬁle)} \rightarrow \text{Ship & Hand Over (CI/CD, Runbook, Case Study)}. Ambiguous business requests only become valuable when translated into explicit constraints, measurable outcomes, and maintainable systems.

   • Prerequisites (Knowledge Graph Gate): Passed Weeks 1–23 Mastery Gates.

3–4 Scaﬀolded Knowledge Points

  1. KP 1: Commercial Discovery, RFC / Statement of Work (SOW) Scoping & Scope-Creep Immunity

        ◦ Primitive: Converting vague business pain points into deterministic Engineering Speciﬁcations (RFCs) with explicit In-Scope Acceptance Criteria and Out-of-Scope Guards.

        ◦ Concept: Never touch Figma or VS Code based on a vague verbal feature list. Deﬁne: (1) The core operational bottleneck and measurable business metric, (2) The exact user roles and permission matrix, (3) The data invariants and oﬄine requirements, (4) Explicit Out-of-Scope boundaries, and (5) A 3-Milestone delivery sign-oﬀ gate
          (Milestone 1: Architecture & Clickable Figma Prototype; Milestone 2: Core Database, Auth & Oﬄine-First Engine; Milestone 3: Polish, Automated Veriﬁcation, CI/CD & Handover).

        ◦ Failure Mode: Jumping straight into coding without a signed-oﬀ Figma prototype and written Out-of-Scope declaration, resulting in endless mid-sprint redesigns and unpaid scope creep.

  2. KP 2: Idempotent Payment/Webhook Fulﬁllment & Transactional Audit Trails

        ◦ Primitive: Cryptographic HMAC-SHA256 webhook signature veriﬁcation (Stripe / Razorpay) + database-level idempotency ledger.

        ◦ Concept: Never trust a client-side payment success callback to unlock paid SaaS features or mark an invoice settled in your database (users can spoof client network calls or lose battery mid-redirect)! Verify the payment provider's cryptographic webhook signature inside a Supabase Deno Edge Function, record the event_id in an
          idempotency_events table inside a serializable Postgres transaction, update the tenant's subscription/invoice state, and let Supabase Realtime + Delta Sync push the unlocked state down to the Flutter app.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Processing duplicate webhook deliveries (payment gateways retry webhooks multiple times on timeout) without checking an idempotency table, double-crediting a customer's account!

  3. KP 3: Production Handover Package (RUNBOOK.md, .env.example, Architecture Case Studies)

Interleaved Review & Active Recall Deck (Full-Curriculum Synthesis)

  1. Q: Why must a commercial SaaS platform never unlock paid features or mark an invoice paid solely from a client-side payment SDK success callback? A: Client callbacks can be spoofed by malicious users or dropped if the user's phone dies right after payment completes. Only a server-side webhook veriﬁed via the payment gateway's
     cryptographic HMAC signature and recorded in an idempotent database transaction is authoritative.

  2. Q: Trace how a single user action ("Approve & Sign Wholesale Invoice #108 Oﬄine") traverses all 4 Phases of your curriculum. A: Phase 1: Captured on an 8pt-grid, accessible AdaptiveScaffold using semantic ThemeExtension tokens and integer paise arithmetic. Phase 4: Signature drawn at 120fps on a RepaintBoundary-isolated
     CustomPainter canvas and exported to PNG inside a background Isolate, with a velocity-preserving SpringSimulation sheet dismissal. Phase 3: Persisted atomically in Drift background SQLite (sync_status = 'pending') + queued in sync_outbox with a UUID v4 idempotency key. Phase 2: When online, OutboxSyncEngine pushes
     via Dio (with Completer-locked JWT refresh and exponential backoﬀ + jitter) to a Supabase RLS-protected RPC, which broadcasts CDC updates over WebSockets to all connected devices.

  3. Q: What are the 5 mandatory sections of a commercial Request for Comments (RFC) / Statement of Work (SOW) that prevent freelance scope creep? A: (1) Executive Business Problem & Measurable Success Metric, (2) System Architecture & Data/Security Boundaries, (3) Explicit Feature Acceptance Criteria (Deﬁnition of Done), (4) Explicit Out-of-
     Scope Declarations, and (5) Milestone Sign-Oﬀ & Payment Schedule.

  4. Q: When diagnosing a production bug, how do you systematically isolate which layer failed using the First-Principles Deconstruction Framework? A: Inspect the 4 foundational mechanics in order: (1) Source of Truth (is the raw data in Postgres / Drift SQLite accurate?), (2) I/O Boundary (did the HTTP/WebSocket serialization, RLS policy, or
     DTO mapper drop/corrupt it?), (3) Transform Pipeline (did the Riverpod Notifier or collection fold compute a wrong derived state?), and (4) Execution Context / Render Tree (did a missing ValueKey, tight BoxConstraint, or stale ref.read prevent the UI from painting it?).
   5. Q: What distinguishes a Senior/Principal Mobile Architect's portfolio from a beginner's tutorial portfolio? A: Instead of showing happy-path UI clones, it proves engineering ownership over failure modes and invariants: 3NF ERDs, PostgreSQL RLS matrices, oﬄine outbox compaction & 3-way conﬂict resolution, < 8ms Impeller frame proﬁles,
      90%+ automated test suites, and clear problem-to-decision case studies.

1. Core Primitives & First-Principles Mental Model
+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                           DART ISOLATE MEMORY & COMPILATION PIPELINE                              |
|                                                                                                   |
| [ Raw UTF-8 Source (.dart) ] ---> [ Dart Front-End (CFE) ] ---> [ Flow-Sensitive Type Analysis ] |
|                                                                                 |                 |
| +------------------------------------------------------------------------------+                  |
| | Sound Null Safety Verified at Compile Time: Object? (Nullable) vs Object (Non-Nullable)         |
| v                                                                                                 |
| [ Execution Runtime: Single-Threaded Isolate ]                                                    |
|     ├── CALL STACK (Value Bindings / Pointers: final, var, local primitives)                      |
|     ├── HEAP MEMORY (Allocated Object Graphs: Lists, Maps, Strings, Custom Instances)             |
|     └── CANONICALIZED CONST POOL (Compile-Time Frozen Singletons: const [1, 2], const Object())   |
+---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

1.1 The 4 Foundational Mechanics

Before writing a line of Dart, anchor every task to these four unalterable runtime mechanics:

   1. Source of Truth (Memory & State): Every variable in Dart is a reference binding stored on the Call Stack that points to an object allocated on the Isolate Heap (or inside the compile-time Canonicalized Constant Pool for const values).

   2. Transform Pipeline: Raw untrusted strings (stdin or ﬁle bytes) pass through validation guards, ﬂow-sensitive type promotion (T? → T), and deterministic integer arithmetic (int paise/cents) to produce validated domain outputs.
   3. Execution Runtime: The Dart VM executes code inside an isolated memory space (Isolate) with its own single-threaded mutator thread and generational Garbage Collector (Young Space Scavenger + Old Space Mark-Sweep).

   4. I/O Boundaries & Failure Modes: External inputs crossing into the program via CLI arguments or stdin.readLineSync() are untrusted and nullable (String?); unvalidated casts (as), forced null assertions (!), or base-2 ﬂoating-point currency math (double) corrupt state at the boundary.

1.2 Primary Architectural Trade-Oﬀs

   • const (Compile-Time Canonicalization) vs. final (Runtime Single-Assignment): const freezes the entire transitive object graph at compile time and deduplicates identical instances to a single memory address (O(1) identity comparison via identical(a, b)), whereas final only locks the stack pointer at runtime while allowing the
     underlying heap collection to mutate.

   • Integer Minor Units (int paise) vs. IEEE 754 Floating-Point (double rupees): double provides hardware speed for scientiﬁc approximations but cannot represent base-10 fractions like 0.1 or 0.01 exactly in binary (0.1 + 0.2 == 0.30000000000000004). Storing currency as integer minor units (₹450.50 → 45050 paise) trades
     minor display-formatting overhead for 100% deterministic ﬁnancial accuracy.

   • Flat Guard Clauses vs. Nested Conditional Pyramids: Validating preconditions at the top of a function and returning early reduces working-memory cognitive load and enables Dart’s ﬂow-sensitive type promotion for the rest of the function body.

1.3 Rabbit Holes to Park in 80_20_parking_lot.md (Do Not Study This Week)

To protect your attention and avoid cognitive overload, explicitly defer these topics until their designated weeks:

   • Do not touch Flutter UI widgets, StatelessWidget, or BuildContext yet (scheduled for Week 5).

   • Do not study Future, async/await, Stream, or Isolate.run yet (scheduled for Weeks 3 and 18).

   • Do not install third-party pub.dev packages (other than test and lints).


2. Curated High-Signal Learning Resources & Book Canon

        ◦ Dart Language Tour: Variables, Operators, Built-in Types, Control Flow, and Error Handling.

Day 1 (Monday — Mode: UNDERSTAND): Memory Models (final vs. const), Stack vs. Heap & Strict Analyzer Setup

   • Daily Focus: How Dart binds variables on the Stack to objects on the Heap, compile-time constant canonicalization, and initializing a strict-mode engineering workspace.

   • Knowledge Point (KP 1.1) — Subgoal Labeling: Binding Mutability vs. Object Mutability

        ◦ Primitive: Stack reference binding vs. Heap object allocation vs. Compile-time canonical pool.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          void main() {
            final runtimeList = <int>[10, 20];
            runtimeList.add(30); // Allowed: heap object mutates; stack pointer is unchanged.

              const compileTimeListA = <int>[10, 20];
              const compileTimeListB = <int>[10, 20];
              print(identical(compileTimeListA, compileTimeListB)); // true (same memory address!)
          }

        ◦ Immediate Variation: Predict on paper what identical(finalListA, finalListB) and compileTimeListA.add(30) produce at compile time vs. runtime.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Declaring const on a variable initialized with a runtime value (DateTime.now()), or mutating a const collection at runtime (UnsupportedError: Cannot add to an unmodifiable list).

Day 2 (Tuesday — Mode: BUILD): Sound Null Safety (Object? vs. Object) & Flow-Sensitive Type Promotion

   • Daily Focus: Dart's bifurcated type hierarchy, null-aware operators (?., ??, ??=), and the exact rules governing when the compiler promotes T? to T.

   • Knowledge Point (KP 1.2) — Subgoal Labeling: Bifurcated Type Hierarchy & Flow Promotion Limits

        ◦ Primitive: Object? is the union of Object (all non-nullable types) and Null; Never is the bottom type at the base of the hierarchy.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          int parseCleanQuantity(String? rawQty) {
            if (rawQty == null) return 0; // Guard clause: rawQty promoted from String? to String!
            final trimmed = rawQty.trim(); // Safe: no ?. needed
            return int.tryParse(trimmed) ?? 0;
          }

        ◦ Immediate Variation: Write a class ReceiptDraft with a nullable ﬁeld String? discountCode and test promoting it inside a method vs. copying it to a local final code = discountCode; variable.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Mutating a local variable inside a closure (() { rawQty = null; }), which immediately de-promotes rawQty back to String? because the compiler cannot guarantee when the closure will execute.

        ◦ Obsidian /00_inbox/ Extraction: How "Dividing the Problem" maps to decomposing a messy invoice string into isolated sub-parsers (SKU parser, Quantity parser, Price parser).

   • Oﬃcial Docs Reading (dart.dev): Read Understanding Null Safety (from top to bottom—pay special attention to the Top and bottom (Object? vs Never) diagram and Flow analysis / Promotion).

   • Physical Notebook 4-Quadrant Active Generation Drill (Close Docs First):

        ◦ Q1 (Type Hierarchy Diagram): Draw Dart’s Sound Null Safety Type Hierarchy from memory (Object? at the top, splitting into Object and Null, branching down to num → int/double, String, Iterable → List, and converging at Never at the bottom).

        ◦ Q2 (Promotion State Table): List the 3 compiler rules where if (x != null) refuses to promote x (1: Public/overridable getter, 2: External/global variable, 3: Local variable mutated inside a closure) and the exact pattern that ﬁxes all three.

        ◦ Q3 (Paper Code): Handwrite the smallest working slice of your weekly project: SkuLineParser.parseSkuAndQty(String? rawLine) that parses "SKU-104 | Qty: 3" into a strongly typed result without using a single ! bang operator or as cast.

Day 3 (Wednesday — Mode: EXPAND): IEEE 754 Floating-Point Traps, Integer Minor-Unit Currency (Paise) & Bitwise/Arithmetic Invariants

   • Daily Focus: Why double corrupts ﬁnancial ledgers, building an integer minor-unit (Paise) currency parser and tax/discount engine, and First Spaced-Repetition Review (Day 3 SRS interval).

   • Knowledge Point (KP 1.3) — Subgoal Labeling: Base-10 Minor-Unit Integer Arithmetic

        ◦ Primitive: 64-bit two's complement int vs. 64-bit IEEE 754 binary ﬂoating-point double (1 sign bit, 11 exponent bits, 52 mantissa bits).

        ◦ Worked Example:

          // BAD: 0.1 + 0.2 == 0.30000000000000004
          // GOOD: Parse "450.50" into 45050 integer paise using string splitting (zero double math!)
          int? tryParseRupeesToPaise(String rawPrice) {
            final cleaned = rawPrice.trim();
            final parts = cleaned.split('.');
            if (parts.isEmpty || parts.length > 2) return null;
            final rupees = int.tryParse(parts[0]);
            if (rupees == null || rupees < 0) return null;
            final paiseStr = parts.length == 2 ? parts[1].padRight(2, '0') : '00';
            if (paiseStr.length > 2) return null; // Reject sub-paise fractions like "450.555"
            final paise = int.tryParse(paiseStr);
            if (paise == null || paise < 0) return null;
            return (rupees * 100) + paise;
          }

        ◦ Immediate Variation: Write the inverse formatter String formatPaiseToRupees(int totalPaise) that outputs "₹450.50" (and handles "₹0.05" with proper zero-padding padLeft(2, '0')) using integer division ~/ and remainder %.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Doing (double.parse("19.99") * 100).toInt(), which evaluates 19.99 * 100 in IEEE 754 binary as 1998.9999999999998 and truncates to 1998 paise (losing 1 paisa on every invoice!).

Passive / Interrupted Shop Track (2–4 hrs, Phone + Physical Notebook Only)

   • 30-Min Book Canon Slice: Read Think Like a Programmer, Chapter 2 (pp. 25–40: Pure Puzzles — Half of a Square, Sideways Triangle, and Luhn Checksum Validation).

        ◦ Obsidian /00_inbox/ Extraction: Note how character-to-digit conversion and modulo % 10 arithmetic allow processing arbitrarily long numeric strings digit-by-digit without ﬂoating-point errors.

   • Oﬃcial Docs Reading (dart.dev): Read Built-in Types → Numbers (int, double, num) and Language → Operators (Arithmetic operators /, ~/, %, Type test operators is, is!).

   • Physical Notebook 4-Quadrant Active Generation Drill (Close Docs First):
                                           1
        ◦ Q1 (Binary Trace): Write out why 10 (0.110 ) is an inﬁnite repeating fraction in base-2 (0.0001100110011 …2 ) just like 13 is 0.3333 … in base-10.

        ◦ Q2 (Tax & Discount Rounding Math): Derive the integer Half-Up rounding formula for applying a basis-point discount or GST percentage (e.g., 18% GST on 45050 paise):

                                                                                                                                                        taxPaise = (netPaise × ratePercent + 50) ∼ / 100
          Verify this formula by hand on paper for 3 real prices from your shop.

        ◦ Q3 (Paper Code): Handwrite tryParseRupeesToPaise(String), formatPaiseToRupees(int), and computeDiscountedTax(int unitPaise, int qty, int discountPct, int gstPct) in your notebook.

   • 5 Mental Model Veriﬁcation Questions (Run via Mobile AI Socratic Tutor Prompt):

Day 4 (Thursday — Mode: INTEGRATE): Flat Guard Clauses, Control Flow Expressions & Structured Error Reporting

   • Daily Focus: Flattening nested if/else arrow code into early-return guard clauses, diﬀerentiating Exception (recoverable boundary failure) vs. Error (programmer bug), and integrating SKU + Qty + Currency + Tax into a complete line-item pipeline.

   • Knowledge Point (KP 1.4) — Subgoal Labeling: Early-Return Guard Clauses & Recoverable Exceptions

        ◦ Primitive: Precondition inversion (if (!valid) return Failure(...)) vs. nested happy-path branching.

        ◦ Worked Example:

          // Instead of throwing raw crashes on messy user input, return structured line results:
          class InvoiceLineResult {
            final String? sku;
            final int? netPaise;
            final String? errorReason;
            final int lineNumber;
            const InvoiceLineResult.success({required this.lineNumber, required String this.sku, required int this.netPaise})
                : errorReason = null;
            const InvoiceLineResult.rejected({required this.lineNumber, required String this.errorReason})
                : sku = null, netPaise = null;
            bool get isValid => errorReason == null;
          }

        ◦ Immediate Variation: Refactor a 4-level nested if/else parser into a linear sequence of 4 single-line guard clauses with maximum indentation depth of 1.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Catching Error (OutOfMemoryError, RangeError, TypeError) silently with a bare catch (e) block and continuing execution in a corrupted state, or letting a single typo on line 4 of a 50-line day-book batch crash the entire program.

Day 5 (Friday — Mode: HARDEN): CLI I/O Streams (dart:io), ASCII Table Formatting & Batch Ledger Aggregation

   • Daily Focus: Connecting the pure domain parser to real CLI I/O boundaries (stdin, stdout, stderr, exit codes), building the complete ASCII Day-Book Summary Table, and running an Adversarial AI Code Audit.

   • Knowledge Point (KP 1.5) — Subgoal Labeling: Pure Core vs. Impure I/O Boundary Shell

        ◦ Primitive: Separating pure, deterministic string/math functions (lib/src/) from impure OS I/O streams (bin/ reading stdin / ﬁles and writing to stdout / stderr).

        ◦ Worked Example:

          // Pure Core (100% testable in memory without OS files or stdin):
          LedgerReport processBatchLines(List<String> rawLines) { ... }
          // Thin Impure CLI Shell (bin/main.dart):
          void main(List<String> args) {
            final lines = readLinesFromFileOrStdin(args);
            final report = processBatchLines(lines);
            stdout.writeln(report.formatAsciiTable());
            if (report.rejectedLines.isNotEmpty) {
              stderr.writeln('Warning: ${report.rejectedLines.length} corrupt lines rejected.');
              exitCode = 1; // Non-zero POSIX exit code for partial failure
            }
          }

        ◦ Immediate Variation: Format right-aligned currency columns (padLeft(12)) and left-aligned SKU columns (padRight(10)) so all pipe borders align to the exact character width.

        ◦ Failure Mode: Calling stdin.readLineSync() or print() directly inside lib/src/invoice_line_parser.dart, making it impossible to run automated unit tests without hanging waiting for keyboard input!

5. Week 1 Spaced-Repetition Flashcard Deck (10 High-Yield Cards)
Add these 10 cards to your Obsidian /wiki/flashcards/week1_deck.md or Anki and review on Days 1, 3, 6, 10, and then weekly:

  1. Front: What is the exact memory diﬀerence between final list = [1, 2]; and const list = [1, 2];? Back: final locks only the Stack pointer at runtime (the Heap list remains mutable via .add()); const canonicalizes and freezes the entire transitive object graph at compile time in the constant pool, making identical(const
     [1, 2], const [1, 2]) == true and throwing UnsupportedError on mutation.
  2. Front: Under what 3 speciﬁc conditions will the Dart compiler refuse to promote a nullable variable T? x to non-nullable T after if (x != null)? Back: (1) x is a public ﬁeld/getter (which a subclass could override to return a diﬀerent value on the second read); (2) x is an external/global variable mutated outside the function; (3) x is a local
     variable mutated inside a closure.

  3. Front: What is the cleanest idiomatic ﬁx when Dart refuses to promote a nullable class property this.discountCode? Back: Copy the property reference into a local final variable (final code = this.discountCode; if (code != null) { ... }). Because the local final cannot be overridden or reassigned, ﬂow analysis promotes
     code immediately.

  4. Front: Why does (19.99 * 100).toInt() evaluate to 1998 instead of 1999, and how do you parse "19.99" into integer paise safely? Back: In IEEE 754 binary ﬂoating-point, 19.99 is stored as 19.9899999999999984368..., so multiplying by 100 and truncating via .toInt() drops a full paisa. Parse by splitting the string on '.' and
     doing pure integer math (int.parse(rupees) * 100) + int.parse(paise.padRight(2, '0')).

  5. Front: What does -405 % 100 return in Dart, and why is that a trap when formatting negative currency amounts? Back: Dart's % operator computes the Euclidean modulo (which is always non-negative for a positive divisor), so -405 % 100 returns 95 instead of 5! Always take final absPaise = paise.abs(); before computing
     absPaise ~/ 100 and absPaise % 100, then prepend '-' if paise < 0.

  6. Front: What is the integer-only formula for applying a ratePercent (e.g., 18 for 18% GST) to int amountPaise with standard half-up commercial rounding? Back: final taxPaise = ((amountPaise * ratePercent) + 50) ~/ 100; (Adding 50 before integer-dividing by 100 rounds any remainder ≥ 0.50 paise up to the next full
     paisa without using double).

  7. Front: What is the role of the Never type at the bottom of Dart's type hierarchy? Back: Never is the uninhabited bottom type (subtype of all types). Functions that always throw an exception or exit (Never fail() => throw ...) have return type Never, proving to the control-ﬂow analyzer that execution never continues past that call.

  8. Front: Why must you use rethrow; instead of throw e; when re-raising an exception inside a catch (e) block? Back: throw e; resets the exception's StackTrace to the current catch block line, destroying the forensic trail of where the bug originally occurred; rethrow; preserves the original StackTrace intact.

  9. Front: What is the architectural diﬀerence between Exception and Error in Dart? Back: Exception represents expected runtime I/O or environment failures (e.g., FormatException, FileSystemException) that boundary code should catch and recover from; Error represents programmer contract violations/bugs (e.g., RangeError,
     TypeError, LateInitializationError) that should be ﬁxed in code, not silently swallowed.
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 N (N +1)
 10. Front: Why is concatenating strings with += inside a loop O(N 2 ) in time and memory, and what should be used instead? Back: Dart Strings are immutable; every result += line allocates a brand-new buﬀer on the Heap and copies all previously accumulated characters (1 + 2 + ⋯ + N =                        2     copies). Using StringBuffer appends
     chunks into a growable internal list and allocates the ﬁnal string once in O(N ) time on .toString().
